Comparison of SAT-Based and ASP-Based Algorithms for Inconsistency Measurement
Isabelle Kuhlmann isabelle.kuhlmann@fernuni-hagen.de, Anna Gessler anna.gessler.work@gmail.com, Vivien Laszlo vivien.laszlo@fernuni-hagen.de

Abstract
We present algorithms based on satisfiability problem (SAT) solving, as well as answer set programming (ASP), for solving the problem of determining inconsistency degrees in propositional knowledge bases. We consider six different inconsistency measures whose respective decision problems lie on the first level of the polynomial hierarchy. Namely, these are the contension, forgetting-based, hitting set, max-distance, sum-distance, and hit-distance inconsistency measures. In an extensive experimental analysis, we compare the SAT-based and ASP-based approaches with each other, as well as with a set of naive baseline algorithms. Our results demonstrate that, overall, both the SAT-based and the ASP-based approaches clearly outperform the naive baseline methods in terms of runtime. The results further show that the proposed ASP-based approaches perform superior to the SAT-based ones with regard to all six inconsistency measures considered in this work. Moreover, we conduct additional experiments to explain the aforementioned results in greater detail.

†
jair-heading:
Vol. 82 (2025), 563-685 — sub: 07/2024, pub: 02/2025

1
Introduction

The handling of conflicting information is a substantial problem in symbolic approaches to Artificial Intelligence.
For instance, different expert opinions could (partially) contradict each other, rule mining algorithms could yield conflicting rules, or data gathered from sensors could be noisy or otherwise distorted.
Thus, inconsistencies can occur in virtually any area of application, and require to be detected and handled.
The field of
inconsistency measurement
(?, ?) provides an analytical perspective on this matter by facilitating the quantitative assessment of the severity of inconsistency in formal knowledge representation formalisms.
Representing the degree of inconsistency as a numerical value may assist automatic reasoning mechanisms on the one hand, and human modellers who aim to identify and compare multiple alternative formalizations on the other hand.
Moreover, such an analysis can be used to identify conflicts, and consequently also help to restore consistency to an inconsistent knowledge base. Inconsistency measures have been used to estimate reliability of agents in multi-agent systems (?), to analyze inconsistencies in news reports (?), to support collaborative software requirements specifications (?), to allow for inconsistency-tolerant reasoning in probabilistic logic (?), to handle inconsistencies in business processes (?, ?), and to monitor and maintain quality in database settings (?, ?).
For a general overview of the subject, see the seminal work by Grant (?) and the edited collection by Grant & Martinez (?).

In the literature, a multitude of different inconsistency measures have been introduced.
Some approaches conceptually rely on minimal inconsistent sets or maximal consistent sets (see, e. g., (?, ?, ?)), others rely on non-classical semantics (see, e. g., (?, ?, ?)), and yet others utilize further properties (see, e. g., (?)).
However—and despite the above list of applications and consequent need for practical working solutions—algorithmic approaches to inconsistency measurement have received only little attention so far.
Ma et al. (?) propose an algorithm that approximates the inconsistency value of a newly proposed inconsistency measure and evaluate it with respect to computational complexity.
Likewise, Xiao & Ma (?) present two new inconsistency measures and perform a complexity analysis on their decision problems, both of which are found to be on the second level of the polynomial hierarchy.
They also develop and evaluate a practically feasible anytime algorithm.

McAreavey et al.
(?) note that there is a lack of practical implementations for inconsistency measures that employ minimal unsatisfiable subsets, and develop and evaluate an algorithm for enumerating such subsets.
Jabbour & Sais (?) describe two algorithms for their newly introduced inconsistency measure, but do not evaluate them with regard to performance or complexity.
Thimm (?) designs and evaluates evolutionary algorithms for two inconsistency measures.
Bertossi (?) proposes an inconsistency measure for databases that can be computed using answer set programming and analyzes its complexity.

As the above overview shows, most algorithmic studies of inconsistency measurement focus on individual inconsistency measures and have failed to address systematic comparisons of algorithms and complexities.
In response, one of the contributions of the survey by Thimm & Wallner (?) was to determine the complexity levels of a large number of inconsistency measures.
The authors concluded that the problem of inconsistency measurement is hard in general, but that there are certain measures which are more suitable candidates for practical applications due to their complexity class.
Based on these findings, for a selection of three different inconsistency measures whose corresponding decisions problems—i. e., deciding whether a certain value is an upper or lower bound of the inconsistency value, or whether it corresponds exactly to the inconsistency value—were found to be on the first level of the polynomial hierarchy, a set of algorithms based on Answer Set Programming (ASP) has been introduced in (?) and (?).
Namely, these measures are the
contension
inconsistency measure (?), the
forgetting-based
inconsistency measure (?), and the
hitting set
inconsistency measures (?) (for their formal definitions, see Section
2
).
Those three measures were not only selected because of their associated complexity class, but also because they each give a different perspective on the inconsistencies in a given knowledge base, meaning that they could each provide different information that could be used to subsequently restore consistency.
The contension inconsistency measure tells us which propositions are involved in a conflict, while the forgetting-based inconsistency measure tells us which occurrences of each proposition are involved in a conflict, and can therefore also point to specific formulas.
In addition, the forgetting-based measure is, to the best of our knowledge, the only inconsistency measure in the literature that is based on the notion of atom occurrences.
The contension inconsistency measure serves as a representative example of inconsistency measures based on non-classical semantics (see, e. g., (?, ?) for other examples of such measures).
The hitting set inconsistency measure offers a further perspective by considering how many different interpretations are minimally needed to (individually) satisfy all formulas.
This measure was originally designed for streaming-based applications, such as Linked Open Data.
With regard to such applications, the development of efficient algorithms is of great interest.

In (?), the three ASP-based approaches were implemented and compared to naive baseline implementations in an experimental evaluation.
As anticipated, the result of the study was that the ASP-based implementations were clearly superior.
Furthermore, in (?), a revised version of the ASP-based approach for the contension inconsistency measure is proposed, in addition to an approach based on
satisfiability problem
(SAT) solving.
The latter is widely used in applications such as hardware verification (?, ?), electronic design automation (?), or cryptanalysis (?, ?).
This, in addition to the fact that there exist highly optimized SAT solvers (see the results of the annual SAT competition
1
1

1

http://www.satcompetition.org/
for an overview), makes it a natural approach for computing inconsistency measures on the first level of the polynomial hierarchy.
Moreover, SAT and ASP have been compared wrt. other applications before (see, e. g., (?, ?)).
The results of the study show that both the ASP and the SAT approach clearly outperform the naive baseline method, but ultimately the ASP approach performs superior to the SAT approach.

In the work at hand, we follow up on (?) by greatly extending the scope of the considered measures and the depth of the experimental evaluation.
For that, we revisit the proposed SAT-based and ASP-based approaches for the contension inconsistency measure and we present SAT-based approaches for the forgetting-based and the hitting set inconsistency measure, as well as revised versions of the corresponding ASP-based approaches introduced in (?).
Moreover, we propose both a SAT-based and an ASP-based approach for each of three different variations of the distance-based inconsistency measure (?), which are likewise on the first level of the polynomial hierarchy.
Further, the distance-based approach offers yet another perspective on the notion of an inconsistency than the three previously discussed measures—Grant & Hunter (?) view the models of the formulas in a knowledge base as points in Euclidian space.
The authors also point out how these measures can be used in applications such as the evaluation of violations of integrity constraints in databases.
We consider the
max-distance
,
sum-distance
, and
hit-distance
inconsistency measures.
Their formal definitions follow, along with those of the other measures, in Section
2
.

Hence, we examine a total of six different inconsistency measures in this work (the contension, the forgetting-based, the hitting set, the max-distance, the sum-distance, and the hit-distance inconsistency measure), and present one SAT-based and one ASP-based approach for each of them.
With regard to each measure, we compare the SAT and the ASP approach to each other, as well as to a naive baseline method in an experimental evaluation.
More precisely, we conduct our experiments on a total of five different data sets.
Whilst two of them were used in the literature before (?, ?), the three other data sets are newly introduced and made publicly available.
Further, we investigate the runtime composition of the SAT-based and the ASP-based approaches in more detail.
In addition to the runtimes, we also record the actual inconsistency values resulting from the different inconsistency measures on the various data sets.
Based on that, we conduct an experiment in which we compare different search strategies wrt. the SAT-based methods.
Overall, the results of our experimental analysis confirm that both the SAT-based and the ASP-based approaches perform superior to the baseline algorithms.
Nevertheless, the results also show that the ASP-based approaches altogether outperform the SAT-based ones.

In the following, we give a concise overview of the structure of this paper.
We first provide the required preliminaries on inconsistency measurement in Section
2
.
Sections
3
and
4
comprise detailed descriptions of our SAT-based and, respectively, ASP-based algorithms.
Section
5
encompasses an evaluation, in which we compare the SAT-based and the ASP-based approaches with each other, and additionally, we draw a comparison with the naive baseline methods.
Section
6
concludes this work by providing a brief summary of our results, as well as an overview of possible future work.

2
Preliminaries

The inconsistency measures examined in this work are all designed to be applied in propositional logic knowledge bases.
A knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
is a finite set of propositional formulas, and we define
\mathbb{K}
as the set of all propositional knowledge bases.
Formulas are constructed by means of the usual connectives
negation
(
\lnot
),
disjunction
(
\lor
), and
conjunction
(
\land
).
Some algorithms defined in Section
3
and
4
use the notion of
subformulas
.
The set of subformulas of a formula
\Phi
is denoted by
\mathsf{sub}(\Phi)
and is inductively defined in the following manner.

Definition 1
.

Let
\Phi
be a propositional formula.
If
\Phi
is a proposition
X
, the only
subformula
of
\Phi
is the proposition itself, meaning
\mathsf{sub}(X)=\{X\}
.
If
\Phi
is a negation
\neg\Psi
, the subformulas
\mathsf{sub}(\neg\Psi)
are given by
\{\neg\Psi\}\cup\mathsf{sub}(\Psi)
.
The subformulas
\mathsf{sub}(\Psi_{1}\bowtie\Psi_{2})
for a formula
\Psi_{1}\bowtie\Psi_{2}
, where
\bowtie\ \in\{\wedge,\vee\}
is a binary operator, are
\{\Psi_{1}\bowtie\Psi_{2}\}\cup\mathsf{sub}(\Psi_{1})\cup\mathsf{sub}(\Psi_{2})
.

Analogously, we define the set of subformulas
\mathsf{sub}(\mathcal{K})
of a knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
:

\mathsf{sub(\mathcal{K})}=\bigcup_{A\in\mathcal{K}}\;\mathsf{sub}(A)

Observe that we denote arbitrary formulas and subformulas as
\Phi
, and formulas that are explicitly elements of a knowledge base as
A
.
If a finer granularity is required, we may use the notation
\Psi
for the subformula of
\Phi
.

We denote the
signature
of a propositional formula or knowledge base, i. e., the (propositional) atoms appearing in it, as
\mathsf{At}(\cdot)
.
Formulas can evaluate to either
true
(abbreviated as
t
) or
false
(abbreviated as
f
).
An
interpretation
is a function
\omega:\mathsf{At}\rightarrow\{t,f\}
that assigns truth values to all atoms.
An interpretation
\omega
satisfies an atom
X\in\mathsf{At}
if and only if
\omega(X)=t
, represented by
\omega\models X
.
For non-atomic formulas, the satisfaction relation is extended recursively according to the truth-valued functions of the connectives as usual (see (?)).

An interpretation that satisfies a formula
\Phi
is also called a
model
of
\Phi
.
For every interpretation
\omega
, if
\omega
is a model of a formula
\Phi_{1}
if and only if
\omega
is a model of another formula
\Phi_{2}
, then
\Phi_{1}
and
\Phi_{2}
are called
logically equivalent
.
A knowledge base is satisfied by an interpretation if all of its formulas are satisfied.
Throughout this paper we denote the set of models of a knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
by
\mathsf{Mod}(\mathcal{K})
and the set of interpretations wrt. the signature
\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
by
\Omega(\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K}))
.

A formula
\Phi
is
inconsistent
if there is no interpretation that satisfies it, meaning
\mathsf{Mod}(\Phi)=\emptyset
.
By extension, a knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
is inconsistent if
\mathsf{Mod}(\mathcal{K})=\emptyset
.
Let
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\infty}
be the set containing all non-negative real numbers and
\infty
.

Definition 2
.

An
inconsistency measure
is a function
\mathcal{I}:\mathbb{K}\to\mathbb{R}^{\infty}_{\geq 0}
which satisfies
\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{K})=0
iff
\mathcal{K}
is consistent, for all
\mathcal{K}\in\mathbb{K}
.

Many different inconsistency measures and properties that characterize these measures have been proposed. To illustrate why there are many ways to define the severity of inconsistency, consider the following example.

Example 1
.

Let
\mathsf{At}=\{\textit{sunny},\textit{cloudy}\}
, where
sunny
represents “it is sunny” and
cloudy
represents “it is cloudy”. The following knowledge bases are inconsistent:

1.

\mathcal{K}_{1}=\{\neg\textit{cloudy},\textit{cloudy},\textit{sunny}\}

2.

\mathcal{K}_{2}=\{\neg\textit{cloudy}\vee\neg\textit{sunny},\textit{cloudy}\vee\textit{sunny},\textit{sunny}\leftrightarrow\textit{cloudy}\}

If our aim was to decide which of these knowledge bases is more severely inconsistent, there are different aspects to consider.
The first knowledge base has a more obvious and easily fixable conflict (remove either
\neg\textit{cloudy}
or
cloudy
), but it also includes a formula that is not involved in any conflict (
sunny
), meaning it contains non-zero information even if the conflict cannot be repaired.
The second knowledge base has a more hidden conflict (all three formulas are required to produce the inconsistency); nevertheless, since all of its formulas are involved in the conflict, there is no “safe” formula that does not participate in any conflict, and the whole knowledge base needs to be discarded if the conflict cannot be repaired.

2.1
The Contension Inconsistency Measure

The contension inconsistency measure
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{c}}}
(?) is based on Priest’s three-valued logic (?).
The latter extends the truth values of propositional logic—
true
(
t
) and
false
(
f
)—by a third value
both
(
b
), also referred to as the inconsistent or paradoxical truth value.
The semantics for this three-valued logic are specified in Table
1
.
Further, a
three-valued interpretation

\omega^{3}:\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})\to\{t,f,b\}
assigns one of the three truth values to each atom in the signature
\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
of the knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
.
Further, a
three-valued model
of a knowledge base is an interpretation
\omega^{3}
that does not assign
f
to any formula.
Therefore, a formula is satisfiable if it evaluates to either
t
or
b
in some interpretation.
The set of three-valued models of a knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
is denoted by
\mathsf{Mod}^{3}(\mathcal{K})
.
For a given three-valued interpretation
\omega^{3}
, we denote the set of atoms that are assigned
b
as the
\mathsf{Conflictbase}(\omega^{3})
.

x

y

\neg x

x\vee y

x\wedge y

t

t

f

t

t

t

b

f

t

b

t

f

f

t

f

t

Table 1
:
Truth table for Priest’s three-valued logic. The rows that differ from classical propositional logic are marked in gray.

Example 2
.

The knowledge base
\mathcal{K}_{3}=\{x,\neg x\}
is unsatisfiable in propositional logic but satisfiable in Priest’s three-valued logic.
The interpretation
\omega^{3}_{1}
on
x
with
\omega^{3}_{1}(x)=b
is a three-valued model for
\mathcal{K}_{3}
with
\mathsf{Conflictbase}(\omega^{3}_{1})=\{x\}
.

Definition 3

(Grant & Hunter (?))
.

The
contension inconsistency measure

\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{c}}}(\mathcal{K}):

\mathbb{K}\to\mathbb{N}_{0}
is defined as

\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{c}}}(\mathcal{K})=\min\{|\mathsf{Conflictbase}(\omega^{3})|\mid\omega^{3}\in\mathsf{Mod}^{3}(\mathcal{K})\}.

In other words, the contension inconsistency value is the minimum number of atoms in a knowledge base that need to be assigned
b
to produce a three-valued model of the knowledge base.
The maximum inconsistency value is
\lvert\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})\rvert
, because there can only be as many
b
assignments as there are atoms in the signature.

Example 3
.

Let
\mathcal{K}_{4}=\{x\wedge y,\neg y\}
.
The interpretation
\omega^{3}_{2}:\{x,y\}\to\{t,b,f\}
with
\omega^{3}_{2}(x)=t
and
\omega^{3}_{2}(y)=b
is a three-valued model for
\mathcal{K}_{4}
with
\mathsf{Conflictbase}(\omega_{2}^{3})=\{y\}
.
There is no model that assigns
b
to
0
atoms, therefore
\min\{|\mathsf{Conflictbase}(\omega_{2}^{3})|\}=|\{y\}|=1
, and hence
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{c}}}(\mathcal{K}_{4})=1
.

2.2
The Forgetting-Based Inconsistency Measure

In order to define the forgetting-based inconsistency measure, we first require the definition of
atom occurrences
in formulas.
Let
\Phi
be a propositional logic formula and
\mathsf{At}(\Phi)
its signature.
An atom
X\in\mathsf{At}(\Phi)
can appear multiple times in
\Phi
.
Each of these appearances is called an
occurrence
of
X
.

Definition 4
.

Let
\mathsf{\#occ}(X,\Phi)
denote the
number of times
X
occurs in
\Phi
.

Example 4
.

Consider
\phi_{1}=x\land y\lor\lnot x\land z
with
\mathsf{At}(\phi_{1})=\{x,y,z\}
.
We can see that
x
appears twice in
\phi_{1}
, and
y
and
z
each appear once.
Consequently,
\mathsf{\#occ}(x,\phi_{1})=2
and
\mathsf{\#occ}(y,\phi_{1})=\mathsf{\#occ}(z,\phi_{1})=1
.

We extend Definition
4
to knowledge bases such that
\mathsf{\#occ}(X,\mathcal{K})
represents the number of times
X
occurs in the formulas of the knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
:

\mathsf{\#occ}(X,\mathcal{K})=\sum_{A\in\mathcal{K}}\mathsf{\#occ}(X,A)

We use superscripts to distinguish between different occurrences of the same atom.
We assume that
X^{l}
refers to the
l
-th occurrence of
X
in the considered formula
\Phi
, or the considered knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
, with
l\in\{1,\ldots,\mathsf{\#occ}(X,\Phi)\}
, or
l\in\{1,\ldots,\mathsf{\#occ}(X,\mathcal{K})\}
, respectively.
We also refer to
l
as the
label
of
X
.

Definition 5
.

We define the set of all
atom occurrences in a formula
\Phi
as

\mathsf{Occ}(\Phi)=\{X_{i}^{l}\mid X_{i}\in\mathsf{At}(\Phi),i\in\{1,\ldots,\lvert\mathsf{At}(\Phi)\rvert\},l\in\{1,\ldots,\mathsf{\#occ}(X_{i},\Phi)\}\}

Example 5
.

Consider again the formula
\phi_{1}=x\land y\lor\lnot x\land z
from Example
4
.
We assign each atom
X
a label
l\in\{1,\ldots,\mathsf{\#occ}(X,\phi_{1})\}
and get

\phi_{1}^{l}=x^{1}\land y^{1}\lor\lnot x^{2}\land z^{1}
.

\mathsf{Occ}(\phi_{1})
contains all (labeled) atom occurrences in
\phi_{1}
, i. e.,

\mathsf{Occ}(\phi_{1})=\{x^{1},x^{2},y^{1},z^{1}\}
.

We analogously extend the definition of
\mathsf{Occ}(\cdot)
to knowledge bases.

The forgetting-based inconsistency measure
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{f}}
as defined by Besnard (?) utilizes a special operation on knowledge bases (
forgetting
) to measure the level of inconsistency.
The idea behind this measure is similar to the one behind the contension inconsistency measure.
However, while the contension measure refers to the atoms in the signature of a knowledge base, the forgetting-based measure refers to individual atom occurrences. An atom occurrence can be “forgotten” by replacing it with
\top
or
\bot
.
We denote the replacement of the
l
-th occurrence of atom
X
in formula
\Phi
by
\top
as
\Phi\mid X^{l}\xrightarrow{\text{f}}\top
(analogously with
\bot
instead of
\top
).
Formally, the forgetting operation is defined as follows.

Definition 6

(Besnard (?))
.

Let
\Phi
be a propositional logic formula containing
\mathsf{\#occ}(X,\Phi)
occurrences of
X\in\mathsf{At}(\Phi)
.
The following substitution of the
l
-th occurrence of
X
is then called
forgetting

X^{l}
in
\Phi
:

\displaystyle\Phi\mid X^{l}\xrightarrow{\text{f}}\top,\bot=(\Phi\mid X^{l}\xrightarrow{\text{f}}\top)\;\lor\;(\Phi\mid X^{l}\xrightarrow{\text{f}}\bot)

For succinct notation we express the formula obtained by forgetting the occurrence
X^{l}
in
\Phi
by writing
\mathcal{F}_{l,X}.\Phi
.

Example 6
.

Consider the formula
\phi_{2}=\neg x^{1}\land\neg y^{1}\land(x^{2}\lor y^{2})\land z^{1}
.
If the second occurrence of
x
(i. e.,
x^{2}
) is forgotten, we obtain:

\begin{split}\phi_{2}\mid x^{2}\xrightarrow{\text{f}}\top,\bot=&(\phi_{2}\mid x^{2}\xrightarrow{\text{f}}\top)\;\lor\;(\phi_{2}\mid x^{2}\xrightarrow{\text{f}}\bot)\\
=&(\neg x^{1}\land\neg y^{1}\land(\top\lor y^{2})\land z^{1})\lor(\neg x^{1}\land\neg y^{1}\land(\bot\lor y^{2})\land z^{1})\\
=&\neg x^{1}\land\neg y^{1}\land z^{1}\end{split}

Observe that the original formula
\phi_{2}
in Example
6
is not satisfiable wrt. classical two-valued semantics, i. e.
\mathsf{Mod}(\phi_{2})=\emptyset
.
However, the formula obtained by forgetting
x^{2}
in
\phi_{2}
, written as
\mathcal{F}_{2,x}.\phi_{2}
, is satisfiable.
In fact, any propositional logic formula
\Phi
can be made consistent by forgetting a sufficient number of atom occurrences. By extension, consistency can be restored for any inconsistent knowledge base
\mathcal{K}\in\mathbb{K}
if a sufficient number of atom occurrences are forgotten.

Definition 7

(Besnard (?))
.

Let
\land_{\mathcal{K}}
be the conjunction of all formulas
A\in\mathcal{K}
.
Based on the forgetting operation, the measure
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{f}}:\mathbb{K}\to\mathbb{N}_{0}
can be defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K})=\min\{n\mid\mathcal{F}_{l_{1},X_{1}}.\mathcal{F}_{l_{2},X_{2}}.\dots.\mathcal{F}_{l_{n},X_{n}}.\land_{\mathcal{K}}\not\models\bot\}

with
X_{i}\in\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
and
l_{i}
being the respective labels with
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

In other words, the inconsistency value
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K})
is the minimum number of atom occurrences which have to be forgotten in order to restore consistency in
\mathcal{K}
.
Forgetting every atom occurrence in a knowledge base yields a trivially consistent knowledge base.
It is thus guaranteed that the inconsistency value
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K})
will not exceed
|\mathsf{Occ}(\mathcal{K})|
.

Example 7
.

Consider the knowledge base
\mathcal{K}_{5}=\{x\land y,x\lor y,z,\lnot x\}
.
After assigning labels to all atom occurrences, we get
\mathcal{K}_{5}^{l}=\{x^{1}\land y^{1},x^{2}\lor y^{2},z^{1},\lnot x^{3}\}
.
Clearly, there is a conflict between
x\land y
and
\lnot x
.
However, if we forget either the first or the third occurrence of
x
(i.e.,
x^{1}
or
x^{3}
),
\mathcal{K}_{5}
becomes consistent.
Since there is no possibility of rendering
\mathcal{K}_{5}
consistent by forgetting less than one atom occurrence,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K}_{5})=1
.

2.3
The Hitting Set Inconsistency Measure

In general, a hitting set is a set that contains at least one element from each set of a given set of sets.
In this work, we use a context-specific form of a hitting set.

Definition 8
.

A set
H\subseteq\Omega(\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K}))
is a
hitting set
of a knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
if for every
A\in\mathcal{K}
there exists an
\omega\in H
with
\omega\models A
.

We use the above notion of a hitting set to define the hitting set inconsistency measure (?).
Let
\mathbb{N}^{\infty}_{0}
denote
\mathbb{N}_{0}\cup\{\infty\}
.

Definition 9

(Thimm (?))
.

The
hitting set inconsistency measure

\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}(\mathcal{K}):\mathbb{K}\to\mathbb{N}^{\infty}_{0}
is defined as

\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}(\mathcal{K})=\min\{\,\lvert H\rvert\mid\text{H is a hitting set of }\mathcal{K}\}-1

with
\min\{\emptyset\}=\infty

\forall\mathcal{K}\in\mathbb{K}\backslash\{\emptyset\}
, and with
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}(\emptyset)=0
.

Intuitively, the hitting set inconsistency measure is based on the idea of searching for the minimum number of interpretations that are required to satisfy all formulas in a given knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
, subtracted by
1
.
If
\mathcal{K}
is consistent, we only need one interpretation (i. e., a model of
\mathcal{K}
).
Thus, if we subtract
1
, we get
0
—the desired inconsistency value for consistent knowledge bases.
If there is a conflict in
\mathcal{K}
, at least
2
interpretations are needed to satisfy all formulas, i. e., the inconsistency value is
>0
.
The more interpretations we need to satisfy all formulas, the more severe the conflict is considered to be.
Note that
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}(\mathcal{K})=\infty
if and only if one of the formulas in
\mathcal{K}
is contradictory.
In this case there exists no hitting set as there exists no interpretation which can satisfy such a formula, and consequently, no number of interpretations can satisfy all formulas in
\mathcal{K}
.
If
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}(\mathcal{K})\neq\infty
then the maximum inconsistency value is
|\mathcal{K}|-1
.
This value occurs when there is a model for every formula in
\mathcal{K}
, but no interpretation is a model for more than one formula.

Example 8
.

Consider again
\mathcal{K}_{4}=\{x\wedge y,\neg y\}
.
Let
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
be interpretations with
\omega_{1}(x)=\omega_{1}(y)=t
, and
\omega_{2}(x)=t
and
\omega_{2}(y)=f
.
Clearly,
\omega_{1}
is a model for
x\land y
, and
\omega_{2}
is a model for
\lnot y
.
There is no single interpretation satisfying both formulas, making
\{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\}
a minimal hitting set of
\mathcal{K}_{4}
.
Therefore,
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}(\mathcal{K}_{4})=|\{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\}|-1=2-1=1
.

Consider another knowledge base
\mathcal{K}_{6}=\{x\wedge\neg x,y,z\}
.
The formula
x\wedge\neg x
is contradictory, consequently there is no hitting set of
\mathcal{K}_{6}
, and
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}(\mathcal{K}_{6})=\infty
.

2.4
Distance-Based Inconsistency Measures

Grant & Hunter (?) proposed several inconsistency measures based on calculating distances between interpretations.
Different distance measures could be used, but we assume that the distance measure is always the Dalal distance
d(\omega,\omega^{\prime})
between two interpretations
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
.
The Dalal distance, also known as Hamming distance, measures distances based on the number of differing digits.
When talking about interpretations, we define the Dalal distance as the number of differing truth value assignments for atoms
X\in\mathsf{At}
between two interpretations
\omega,\omega^{\prime}
.

Definition 10
.

The
Dalal distance

d_{dalal}
between two interpretations
\omega,\omega^{\prime}
is defined as

d_{dalal}(\omega,\omega^{\prime})=\lvert\{X\in\mathsf{At}\mid\omega(X)\neq\omega^{\prime}(X)\}\rvert

Additionally, we define the Dalal distance between a set of interpretations
I\subseteq\Omega(\mathsf{At})
and a single interpretation
\omega
as the smallest distance between some interpretation in
I
and the interpretation
\omega
.

Definition 11
.

The Dalal distance
d_{dalal}
between a
set of interpretations

I
and an
individual
interpretation
\omega
is defined as

d_{dalal}(I,\omega)=\min_{\omega^{\prime}\in I}(\omega^{\prime},\omega),\text{ with }d_{dalal}(\emptyset,\omega)=\infty

We now define three distance-based inconsistency measures.
The general idea behind all three measures is to find an interpretation that has an “optimal” distance to the models of the individual formulas in a given knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
.
The definition of “optimal” differs for each measure.

The
max-distance
measure calculates the interpretation with the smallest maximum distance to the models of each formula in
\mathcal{K}
.
The inconsistency value is equal to the aforementioned smallest maximum distance.
In case that
\mathcal{K}
contains a contradictory formula
A_{\bot}
, i. e.,
\mathsf{Mod}(A_{\bot})=\emptyset
, the maximum distance will always be
\infty
, due to
d_{dalal}(\emptyset,\omega)=\infty
for any
\omega\in\Omega(\mathsf{At})
.
Hence, the minimal maximum distance is
\infty
, and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K})=\infty
.

Definition 12

(Grant & Hunter (?))
.

The
max-distance inconsistency measure

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K}):\mathbb{K}\to\mathbb{N}^{\infty}_{0}
is defined as

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K})=\min\{\max_{A\in\mathcal{K}}d_{dalal}(\mathsf{Mod}(A),\omega)\mid\omega\in\Omega(\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K}))\}

The
sum-distance
measure calculates an interpretation
\omega
such that the sum of
\omega
’s distances to the models of each formula in
\mathcal{K}
is minimal.
The inconsistency value is equal to the aforementioned smallest sum of distances.
Note that in the case that
\mathcal{K}
contains a contradictory formula
A_{\bot}
, each sum will add up to
\infty
, and thus
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K})=\infty
.

Definition 13

(Grant & Hunter (?))
.

The
sum-distance inconsistency measure

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K}):\mathbb{K}\to\mathbb{N}^{\infty}_{0}
is defined as

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K})=\min\{\Sigma_{A\in\mathcal{K}}d_{dalal}(\mathsf{Mod}(A),\omega)\mid\omega\in\Omega(\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K}))\}

The
hit-distance
measure calculates an interpretation such that the number of distances greater than
0
to the models of each formula is minimal.
A different, simpler characterization of this measure (?), shows that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\text{hit}}
is equal to the minimum number of formulas that need to be removed from a knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
in order to make it consistent.

Definition 14

(Grant & Hunter (?))
.

The
hit-distance inconsistency measure

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\text{hit}}(\mathcal{K}):\mathbb{K}\to\mathbb{N}_{0}
is defined as

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\text{hit}}(\mathcal{K})=\min\{\lvert\{A\in\mathcal{K}\mid d_{dalal}(\mathsf{Mod}(A),\omega)>0\}\rvert\mid\omega\in\Omega(\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K}))\}

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K})
and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K})
take the value
\infty
if and only if one of the formulas in
\mathcal{K}
is inconsistent individually.
For the non-infinity case, the maximum value of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K})
is
\lvert\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})\rvert
, because the distance is based on the number of differing atom assignments which cannot exceed the number of atoms.
For the non-infinity case of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K})
, the maximum value is
\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert\cdot\lvert\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})\rvert
, because in the worst case, each distance
d_{dalal}(\mathsf{Mod}(A),\omega)
for all formulas
A\in\mathcal{K}
is maximal.

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\text{hit}}
cannot take the value
\infty
, because the value of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\text{hit}}
is the number of distances greater than
0
rather than a distance itself.
The maximum value of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\text{hit}}
is therefore
|\mathcal{K}|
.

Example 9
.

Consider again
\mathcal{K}_{4}=\{x\wedge y,\neg y\}
.
For simplified readability, we name the formulas:
x\wedge y=:\alpha_{1}
and
\neg y=:\alpha_{2}
.
The possible interpretations for
\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K}_{4})
are
\Omega(\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K}_{4}))=\{\omega_{0},\omega_{x},\omega_{y},\omega_{xy}\}
with:

\displaystyle\omega_{0}(x)=\omega_{0}(y)=f

\displaystyle\omega_{x}(x)=t,\omega_{x}(y)=f

\displaystyle\omega_{y}(x)=f,\omega_{y}(y)=t

\displaystyle\omega_{xy}(x)=\omega_{xy}(y)=t

The models for the formulas in
\mathcal{K}_{4}
are
\mathsf{Mod}(\alpha_{1})=\{\omega_{xy}\}
and
\mathsf{Mod}(\alpha_{2})=\{\omega_{0},\omega_{x}\}
.
We now calculate
d_{dalal}
for all formulas in
\mathcal{K}_{4}
and all interpretations in
\Omega(\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K}_{4}))
:

\displaystyle d_{dalal}(\mathsf{Mod}(\alpha_{1}),\omega_{0})=2

\displaystyle\qquad d_{dalal}(\mathsf{Mod}(\alpha_{2}),\omega_{0})=0

\displaystyle d_{dalal}(\mathsf{Mod}(\alpha_{1}),\omega_{x})=1

\displaystyle\qquad d_{dalal}(\mathsf{Mod}(\alpha_{2}),\omega_{x})=0

\displaystyle d_{dalal}(\mathsf{Mod}(\alpha_{1}),\omega_{y})=1

\displaystyle\qquad d_{dalal}(\mathsf{Mod}(\alpha_{2}),\omega_{y})=1

\displaystyle d_{dalal}(\mathsf{Mod}(\alpha_{1}),\omega_{xy})=0

\displaystyle\qquad d_{dalal}(\mathsf{Mod}(\alpha_{2}),\omega_{xy})=1

The max-distance measure
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}
looks at the maximum distances per interpretation, i. e.,
\max_{\alpha\in\mathcal{K}}d_{dalal}(\mathsf{Mod}(\alpha),\omega)
.
Here, the set of maximal distances consists of:

\displaystyle d_{dalal}(\mathsf{Mod}(\alpha_{1}),\omega_{0})=2

\displaystyle d_{dalal}(\mathsf{Mod}(\alpha_{1}),\omega_{x})=1

\displaystyle d_{dalal}(\mathsf{Mod}(\alpha_{1}),\omega_{y})=1

\displaystyle d_{dalal}(\mathsf{Mod}(\alpha_{2}),\omega_{xy})=1

The value
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K}_{4})
is the minimum of those maxima, meaning

d_{dalal}(\mathsf{Mod}(\alpha_{1}),\omega_{x})=1,

or one of the other maxima with value
1
.
Therefore,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K}_{4})=1
.

For the sum-distance inconsistency measure
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}
, we sum up the distances for each interpretation:

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\alpha\in\mathcal{K}_{4}}d_{dalal}(\mathsf{Mod}(\alpha),\omega_{0})=2

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\alpha\in\mathcal{K}_{4}}d_{dalal}(\mathsf{Mod}(\alpha),\omega_{x})=1

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\alpha\in\mathcal{K}_{4}}d_{dalal}(\mathsf{Mod}(\alpha),\omega_{y})=2

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\alpha\in\mathcal{K}_{4}}d_{dalal}(\mathsf{Mod}(\alpha),\omega_{xy})=1.

The value of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K}_{4})
is the minimum of the above sums.
Consequently,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K}_{4})=1
.

For the hit-distance inconsistency measure
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\text{hit}}
, we count the number of distances greater than 0 for each interpretation:

\displaystyle\lvert\{\alpha\in\mathcal{K}_{4}\mid d_{dalal}(\mathsf{Mod}(\alpha),\omega_{0})>0\}\rvert=1

\displaystyle\lvert\{\alpha\in\mathcal{K}_{4}\mid d_{dalal}(\mathsf{Mod}(\alpha),\omega_{x})>0\}\rvert=1

\displaystyle\lvert\{\alpha\in\mathcal{K}_{4}\mid d_{dalal}(\mathsf{Mod}(\alpha),\omega_{y})>0\}\rvert=2

\displaystyle\lvert\{\alpha\in\mathcal{K}_{4}\mid d_{dalal}(\mathsf{Mod}(\alpha),\omega_{xy})>0\}\rvert=1

The inconsistency value is the minimum of those counts:
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\text{hit}}(\mathcal{K}_{4})=1
.

3
SAT-Based Algorithms for Selected Inconsistency Measures

By now we have established a fundamental overview of inconsistency measurement in general, and we defined the six particular inconsistency measures considered in this work.
Building on that, in the section at hand, we describe how to use SAT encodings to determine inconsistency values.

3.1
Satisfiability Solving

A major problem in the realm of propositional logic is the
Boolean satisfiability problem
, which is one of the most-studied problems of computer science, and which is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete (?).
The input of the Boolean satisfiability problem is a formula in
conjunctive normal form
(CNF), which is a conjunction of clauses.
Note that we do not restrict our input knowledge bases for inconsistency measurement to CNF, however, every propositional formula can be converted into an equivalent formula in CNF (see (?) for a proof).
Naively, this conversion can be done using Boolean transformation rules, but the resulting formulas are sometimes exponentially larger than the original formula.
Using the Tseitin method (?), every formula can be converted into an equisatisfiable CNF formula with only a linear increase in size.

Definition 15
.

The
Boolean satisfiability problem
(SAT) is the problem of deciding if there exists an interpretation that satisfies a given propositional formula.

•

Input:
formula
\Phi
in CNF

•

Output:

true
iff
\mathsf{Mod}(\Phi)\neq\emptyset
,
false
otherwise

A SAT solver is a program that solves SAT for a given formula.
There exist many high-performance SAT solvers (for an overview, see the results of the recurring SAT competition
2
2

2

http://www.satcompetition.org/
).
A consequence of the
\mathsf{NP}
-completeness of SAT is that SAT solvers can also be used to solve other
\mathsf{NP}
-complete problems if we transform them into SAT problems.

In order to use SAT solving for the problem at hand, we need a way to encode the concept that some value is an upper limit.

Cardinality constraints
represent that at least, at most, or exactly some number
k
out of a set of propositional atoms are allowed to be true.
Using the formal definition of Abio et al. (?), we define that a
cardinality constraint
is a constraint of the form

X_{1}+\ldots+X_{n}\bowtie k

where
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
are propositional atoms,
\bowtie\ \in\{<,\leq,=,\geq,>\}
is an operator, and
k
is a natural number.
The meaning of the
+
operator in this context is that for every true atom the number
1
is added and for every false atom the number
0
is added, thereby counting the number of true atoms.
Constraints of the form

X_{1}+\ldots+X_{n}\leq k

are informally referred to as
at-most-
k
constraints
.
Such a constraint is true if and only if
\leq k
atoms out of the set
\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\}
are true.
The direct approach to encode cardinality constraints is to enumerate all possible atom assignments that satisfy the constraints.
For small knowledge bases the resulting encodings can be compact; however, in general, there are
\binom{n}{k+1}
subsets of size
k+1
, meaning we generate
\binom{n}{k+1}
clauses.
It is inefficient for larger inputs because the size of the constraint grows exponentially with
n
.
Several more efficient methods to encode cardinality constraints more efficiently have been developed.
In the implementations used for our experimental evaluation (Section
5
), we use the
sequential counter encoding
method (?) which only generates
n\cdot k
clauses.

In the following sections, we use the notation
\mathrm{at\_most}(k,\mathbb{X})
where
\mathbb{X}
is a set of atoms
\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\}
to denote the constraint
X_{1}+\ldots+X_{n}\leq k
.

3.2
Scheme for SAT-Based Algorithms for Inconsistency Measures

This section proposes a binary search algorithm for the computation of the inconsistency value of a given knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
wrt. one of the inconsistency measures considered in this work, i. e., the computation of
\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{K})
with
\mathcal{I}\in\{\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{c}}},\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{f}},\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}},\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max},\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma},\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\text{hit}}\}
.
Our approach follows similar procedures that have been proposed in the literature (see, e.g., (?)).
So the objective of our algorithm is to solve the function problem
\textsc{value}_{\mathcal{I}}
as formalized in (?).

\textsc{value}_{\mathcal{I}}

Input:

\mathcal{K}\in\mathbb{K}

Output:

value of
\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{K})

All inconsistency measures considered in this paper have a clearly defined range as established in their descriptions in Section
2
(see also Table
2
).
This clearly defined search space prompts the use of a binary search procedure. The range is searched by the algorithm and for each iteration of the search, a call to a SAT-solver is made, to decide which half of the range needs to be searched in the following iteration.
The problem that is solved at each iteration of the search procedure is the decision problem
\textsc{upper}_{\mathcal{I}}
(?), meaning the problem of deciding whether a given value
u
is an upper bound of the inconsistency value of a given knowledge base.

\textsc{upper}_{\mathcal{I}}

Input:

\mathcal{K}\in\mathbb{K},

u\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

Output:

true
iff
\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{K})\leq u

\mathcal{I}

Maximum non-
\infty
value

\infty
possible?

\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{c}}}

\lvert\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})\rvert

No

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{f}}

\lvert\mathsf{Occ}(\mathcal{K})\rvert

No

\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}

\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert-1

Yes

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}

\lvert\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})\rvert

Yes

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}

\lvert\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})\rvert\cdot\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert

Yes

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\text{hit}}

\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert

No

Table 2
:
Ranges of possible values of each inconsistency measure considered in this work. The minimum value is
0
in all cases.

For all inconsistency measures presented in Section
2
, the problem
\textsc{upper}_{\mathcal{I}}
is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete as shown in (?), and is thus reducible to SAT.
Therefore, the idea is to find SAT encodings
S:\mathbb{K}\times\mathbb{N}_{0}\to\mathbb{K}
for
\textsc{upper}_{\mathcal{I}}
for all inconsistency measures, that satisfiy the requirement that
(\mathcal{K},u)
is a positive instance of
\textsc{upper}_{\mathcal{I}}
if and only if
S(\mathcal{K},u)
is a positive instance of SAT.
At each search step, we compute the encoding for the current potential upper bound
u
and make a call to a SAT solver to check if
u
is in fact an upper limit of the inconsistency value, until the exact value is found.
If no value is found,
\infty
is assumed to be the inconsistency value for the measures
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}
,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}
, and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}
.
With regard to the other measures (
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{c}}}
,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{f}}
, and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\text{hit}}
), the binary search procedure is guaranteed to return a finite value after
\log_{2}(n+1)
steps, with
n
being the maximally possible finite value.

Note that the iterative SAT-based approach described in this section is essentially a naive MaxSAT approach.
Although the literature offers many different dedicated MaxSAT solvers (see, e. g., (?) for a recent overview), which are based on several different optimization algorithms, we decided to rely on classical SAT solvers for this work. In particular, the focus of this work is on the presented encodings; a detailed investigation of the various optimization algorithms would exceed the scope of this paper (note, however, that we briefly touch upon the topic of optimization wrt. SAT in Section
5.3.4
and wrt. ASP in Section
5.3.5
).

3.3
The Contension Inconsistency Measure

Beginning with this section, we describe how to construct the SAT encodings that are used in the binary search procedure described in the previous section for each specific inconsistency measure.
Note that the encoding for the contension inconsistency measure covered in this section was already proposed in (?).
However, for the sake of completeness, we describe it once more.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a propositional knowledge base and
u
an integer representing a possible upper bound for the contension inconsistency value
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{c}}}(\mathcal{K})
.
We present a SAT encoding for
\textsc{upper}_{\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{c}}}}
, denoted
S_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{K},u)
, which is defined by the components (SC1)–(SC17).

Recall that
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{c}}}(\mathcal{K})
is defined by the minimal number of atoms in
\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
that need to be set to
b
in order for
\mathcal{K}
to become consistent in Priest’s three-valued logic.
To encode the three-valued logic in propositional logic, we use additional variables.
First of all, for every atom
X
in the original signature
\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
, we use three new atoms
X_{t}
,
X_{b}
,
X_{f}
(SC1) to represent the three truth values
t
,
b
,
f
. We need to assure that out of each triple of new atoms that represent the three possible truth values of the original atom
X
, exactly one is true.
We represent this by adding the following formula for every
X\in\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
to the encoding
S_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{K},u)
:

\displaystyle(X_{t}\vee X_{f}\vee X_{b})\wedge(\neg X_{t}\vee\neg X_{f})\wedge(\neg X_{t}\vee\neg X_{b})\wedge(\neg X_{b}\vee\neg X_{f})

(SC3)

To model three-valued satisfiability, we recursively represent the evaluation of formulas in three-valued logic (see the semantics described in Section
2.1
).
To achieve this, three variables
v_{\Phi}^{t},v_{\Phi}^{f},v_{\Phi}^{b}
(SC2) are used for every subformula
\Phi
in every formula
A\in\mathcal{K}
to represent each of the three possible valuations of
\Phi
.
For each of those valuation atoms we add an equivalence that defines the evaluations based on the operator of the subformula.
To represent all possible formulas, we need to encode the operators
\land
,
\lor
, and
\lnot
.

We encode conjunctions
\Phi_{c}=\Psi_{c,1}\land\Psi_{c,2}
by adding the following formulas to
S_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{K},u)
:

\displaystyle v_{\Phi_{c}}^{t}\leftrightarrow v_{\Psi_{c,1}}^{t}\wedge v_{\Psi_{c,2}}^{t}

(SC4)

\displaystyle v_{\Phi_{c}}^{f}\leftrightarrow v_{\Psi_{c,1}}^{f}\vee v_{\Psi_{c,2}}^{f}

(SC5)

\displaystyle v_{\Phi_{c}}^{b}\leftrightarrow(\neg v_{\Psi_{c,1}}^{t}\vee\neg v_{\Psi_{c,2}}^{t})\wedge\neg v_{\Psi_{c,1}}^{f}\wedge\neg v_{\Psi_{c,2}}^{f}

(SC6)

Analogously, disjunctions
\Phi_{d}=\Psi_{d,1}\lor\Psi_{d,2}
are encoded as follows:

\displaystyle v_{\Phi_{d}}^{t}\leftrightarrow v_{\Psi_{d,1}}^{t}\vee v_{\Psi_{d,2}}^{t}

(SC7)

\displaystyle v_{\Phi_{d}}^{f}\leftrightarrow v_{\Psi_{d,1}}^{f}\wedge v_{\Psi_{d,2}}^{f}

(SC8)

\displaystyle v_{\Phi_{d}}^{b}\leftrightarrow(\neg v_{\Psi_{d,1}}^{f}\vee\neg v_{\Psi_{d,2}}^{f})\wedge\neg v_{\Psi_{d,1}}^{t}\wedge\neg v_{\Psi_{d,2}}^{t}

(SC9)

To represent a negated formula
\Phi_{n}=\neg\Psi_{n}
, we create new variables representing the three evaluations of
\Phi_{n}
, meaning
v_{\Phi_{n}}^{t}
,
v_{\Phi_{n}}^{f}
and
v_{\Phi_{n}}^{b}
, and encode the evaluation of those cases by adding the following formulas to
S_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{K},u)
:

\displaystyle v_{\Phi_{n}}^{t}\leftrightarrow v_{\Psi_{n}}^{f}

(SC10)

\displaystyle v_{\Phi_{n}}^{f}\leftrightarrow v_{\Psi_{n}}^{t}

(SC11)

\displaystyle v_{\Phi_{n}}^{b}\leftrightarrow v_{\Psi_{n}}^{b}

(SC12)

Thus, (SC10) encodes that the formula
\Phi_{n}=\neg\Psi_{n}
evaluates to
t
if
\Psi_{n}
evaluates to
f
.
In the same fashion,
\Phi_{n}
evaluates to
f
if
\Psi_{n}
is
t
(SC11), and
\Phi_{n}
evaluates to
b
if
\Psi_{n}
is also
b
(SC12).
Further, we add the following formulas for each subformula
\Phi_{a}
which represents an individual atom
X
:

\displaystyle v_{\Phi_{a}}^{t}\leftrightarrow X_{t}

(SC13)

\displaystyle v_{\Phi_{a}}^{f}\leftrightarrow X_{f}

(SC14)

\displaystyle v_{\Phi_{a}}^{b}\leftrightarrow X_{b}

(SC15)

We also add a formula to
S_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{K},u)
that represents when a formula
A\in\mathcal{K}
becomes true.
This is the case when the subformula that contains the whole formula evaluates to
t
or
b
:

\displaystyle v_{A}^{t}\vee v_{A}^{b}

(SC16)

Finally, we add a cardinality constraint representing that at most
u
of the
b
-atoms are allowed to be true.
Let
\mathsf{At}_{b}
be the set of
b
-atoms of
\mathcal{K}
, i. e.,

\mathsf{At}^{\mathcal{K}}_{b}=\{X_{b}\mid X\in\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})\}
.
We add the following cardinality constraint to
S_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{K},u)
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{at\_most}(u,\mathsf{At}^{\mathcal{K}}_{b})

(SC17)

S_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is finally comprised of the signature and the formulas presented in (SC1)–(SC17) (see Encoding
1
in Appendix
A.1.1
for an overview).
The following result establishes that this encoding faithfully implements
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{c}}}
.
The proof of the theorem below is provided in Appendix
A.1.1
.

Theorem 1
.

For a given value
u
, the encoding
S_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is satisfiable if and only if
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{c}}}(\mathcal{K})\leq u
.

Example 10
.

We illustrate the construction of
S_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{K},u)
for the following knowledge base:

\mathcal{K}_{7}=\left\{\underbrace{\overbrace{x}^{\phi_{1,1}}\land\overbrace{y}^{\phi_{1,2}}}_{\alpha_{1}},\quad\underbrace{\overbrace{x}^{\phi_{2,1}}\lor\overbrace{y}^{\phi_{2,2}}}_{\alpha_{2}},\quad\underbrace{\lnot\overbrace{x}^{\phi_{3}}}_{\alpha_{3}}\right\}

In this example, we aim to construct
S_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{K}_{7},1)
, i. e., a SAT encoding which returns true if and only if
1
is an upper bound of the contension inconsistency value of
\mathcal{K}_{7}
.

As
\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K}_{7})=\{x,y\}
, we require
2\cdot 3=6
new atoms (SC1):

x_{t},x_{b},x_{f},\qquad y_{t},y_{b},y_{f}

We also have a total of
8
(sub)formulas, so we need
8\cdot 3=24
corresponding atoms (SC2):

\displaystyle v^{t}_{\alpha_{1}},v^{b}_{\alpha_{1}},v^{f}_{\alpha_{1}},\qquad v^{t}_{\alpha_{2}},v^{b}_{\alpha_{2}},v^{f}_{\alpha_{2}},\qquad v^{t}_{\alpha_{3}},v^{b}_{\alpha_{3}},v^{f}_{\alpha_{3}},

\displaystyle v^{t}_{\phi_{1,1}},v^{b}_{\phi_{1,1}},v^{f}_{\phi_{1,1}},\qquad v^{t}_{\phi_{1,2}},v^{b}_{\phi_{1,2}},v^{f}_{\phi_{1,2}},

\displaystyle v^{t}_{\phi_{2,1}},v^{b}_{\phi_{2,1}},v^{f}_{\phi_{2,1}},\qquad v^{t}_{\phi_{2,2}},v^{b}_{\phi_{2,2}},v^{f}_{\phi_{2,2}},

\displaystyle v^{t}_{\phi_{3}},v^{b}_{\phi_{3}},v^{f}_{\phi_{3}}

We add the following constraints to the encoding
S_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{K}_{7},u)
via (SC3):

\displaystyle(x_{t}\vee x_{f}\vee x_{b})\wedge(\neg x_{t}\vee\neg x_{f})\wedge(\neg x_{t}\vee\neg x_{b})\wedge(\neg x_{b}\vee\neg x_{f})

\displaystyle(y_{t}\vee y_{f}\vee y_{b})\wedge(\neg y_{t}\vee\neg y_{f})\wedge(\neg y_{t}\vee\neg y_{b})\wedge(\neg y_{b}\vee\neg y_{f})

We encode the first formula
\alpha_{1}=\phi_{1,1}\land\phi_{1,2}
, which is a conjunction, by adding the following formulas corresponding to (SC4)–(SC6):

\displaystyle v_{\alpha_{1}}^{t}\leftrightarrow v_{\phi_{1,1}}^{t}\wedge v_{\phi_{1,2}}^{t}

\displaystyle v_{\alpha_{1}}^{f}\leftrightarrow v_{\phi_{1,1}}^{f}\vee v_{\phi_{1,2}}^{f}

\displaystyle v_{\alpha_{1}}^{b}\leftrightarrow(\neg v_{\phi_{1,1}}^{t}\vee\neg v_{\phi_{1,2}}^{t})\wedge\neg v_{\phi_{1,1}}^{f}\wedge\neg v_{\phi_{1,2}}^{f}

We encode the second formula
\alpha_{2}=\phi_{2,1}\lor\phi_{2,2}
, which is a disjunction, by adding the formulas corresponding to (SC7)–(SC9):

\displaystyle v_{\alpha_{2}}^{t}\leftrightarrow v_{\phi_{2,1}}^{t}\vee v_{\phi_{2,2}}^{t}

\displaystyle v_{\alpha_{2}}^{f}\leftrightarrow v_{\phi_{2,1}}^{f}\wedge v_{\phi_{2,2}}^{f}

\displaystyle v_{\alpha_{2}}^{b}\leftrightarrow(\neg v_{\phi_{2,1}}^{f}\vee\neg v_{\phi_{2,2}}^{f})\wedge\neg v_{\phi_{2,1}}^{t}\wedge\neg v_{\phi_{2,2}}^{t}

The last formula (
\alpha_{3}=\lnot\phi_{3}
), which is a negation, is modeled by adding the following formulas via (SC10)–(SC12):

\displaystyle v_{\alpha_{3}}^{t}\leftrightarrow v_{\phi_{3}}^{f}

\displaystyle v_{\alpha_{3}}^{f}\leftrightarrow v_{\phi_{3}}^{t}

\displaystyle v_{\alpha_{3}}^{b}\leftrightarrow v_{\phi_{3}}^{b}

We add formulas to encode that
\phi_{1,1}
,
\phi_{1,2}
,
\phi_{2,1}
,
\phi_{2,2}
, and
\phi_{3}
are subformulas consisting of individual atoms, following (SC13)–(SC15):

\displaystyle\phi^{t}_{1,1}\leftrightarrow x_{t}\qquad

\displaystyle\phi^{t}_{1,2}\leftrightarrow y_{t}\qquad

\displaystyle\phi^{t}_{2,1}\leftrightarrow x_{t}\qquad

\displaystyle\phi^{t}_{2,2}\leftrightarrow y_{t}\qquad

\displaystyle\phi^{t}_{3}\leftrightarrow x_{t}

\displaystyle\phi^{f}_{1,1}\leftrightarrow x_{f}\qquad

\displaystyle\phi^{f}_{1,2}\leftrightarrow y_{f}\qquad

\displaystyle\phi^{f}_{2,1}\leftrightarrow x_{f}\qquad

\displaystyle\phi^{f}_{2,2}\leftrightarrow y_{f}\qquad

\displaystyle\phi^{f}_{3}\leftrightarrow x_{f}

\displaystyle\phi^{b}_{1,1}\leftrightarrow x_{b}\qquad

\displaystyle\phi^{b}_{1,2}\leftrightarrow y_{b}\qquad

\displaystyle\phi^{b}_{2,1}\leftrightarrow x_{b}\qquad

\displaystyle\phi^{b}_{2,2}\leftrightarrow y_{b}\qquad

\displaystyle\phi^{b}_{3}\leftrightarrow x_{b}

For each formula in
\mathcal{K}
we additionally add a constraint wrt. (SC16):

\displaystyle v_{\alpha_{1}}^{t}\vee v_{\alpha_{1}}^{b}

\displaystyle v_{\alpha_{2}}^{t}\vee v_{\alpha_{2}}^{b}

\displaystyle v_{\alpha_{3}}^{t}\vee v_{\alpha_{3}}^{b}

Finally, via (SC17), we add the cardinality constraint
\mathrm{at\_most}(1,\{x_{b},y_{b}\})
.

3.4
The Forgetting-Based Inconsistency Measure

Let
\mathcal{K}
again be a propositional knowledge base and
u
an integer representing a candidate for an upper bound of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K})
.
Recall that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K})
is defined as the minimal number of atom occurrences which need to be forgotten in order to resolve all inconsistencies in
\mathcal{K}
.
We construct an encoding
S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K},u)
that represents
\textsc{upper}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{f}}}
for the instance
(\mathcal{K},u)
.

To begin with, we label the given knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
to retrieve
\mathsf{Occ}(\mathcal{K})
.
For each labeled atom occurrence
X^{l}\in\mathsf{Occ}(\mathcal{K})
we also add two atoms
t_{X,l}
and
f_{X,l}
to the signature (SF1).
Further, for each formula
A\in\mathcal{K}
a corresponding constraint
A^{\prime}
is created and added to
S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K},u)
.

A^{\prime}
is an extension of
A
, gained by substituting every atom occurrence
X^{l}
in
A
with the subformula
(t_{X,l}\lor X)\land\neg f_{X,l}
.
Assigning truth values to the new atoms
t_{X,l}
,
f_{X,l}
models the forgetting operation on
X^{l}
in
A
.
More precisely, setting
t_{X,l}
to true represents forgetting
X^{l}
and replacing it with
\top
, while setting
f_{X,l}
to true represents forgetting
X^{l}
and replacing it with
\bot
.
We define
\Phi_{X,l}
to be the subformula
X^{l}
is substituted with:

\displaystyle\Phi_{X,l}:=(t_{X,l}\lor X)\land\neg f_{X,l}

(SF2)

•

If
t_{X,l}
is set to true and
f_{X,l}
is set to false, then
\Phi_{X,l}
becomes true, regardless of the truth value assigned to
X
.
This represents the case in which
X^{l}
has been forgotten and was replaced by
\top
.

•

If
t_{X,l}
is set to false and
f_{X,l}
is set to true, then
\Phi_{X,l}
becomes false, regardless of the truth value assigned to
X
.
This represents the case in which
X^{l}
has been forgotten and was replaced by
\bot
.

•

If both
t_{X,l}
and
f_{X,l}
are set to false, then the truth value of
\Phi_{X,l}
is equal to the truth value assigned to
X
.
This represents the case in which
X^{l}
has not been forgotten.

The case in which both
t_{X,l}
and
f_{X,l}
are set to true holds no meaning in the chosen representation, as this would imply that
X^{l}
had somehow been replaced by
\top
and
\bot
simultaneously.
This invalid state needs to be avoided, which motivates the addition of constraints that prevent
t_{X,l}
and
f_{X,l}
from both being set to true at the same time:

\displaystyle\neg t_{X,l}\lor\neg f_{X,l}

(SF3)

Finally, an at-most-
u
constraint is added to limit the number of occurrences that can be forgotten.
Let
\mathsf{At}_{\mathrm{forget}}
denote the set of all atoms introduced by substitutions of atom occurrences in formulas
A\in\mathcal{K}
, i. e., the set of all
t_{X,l},f_{X,l}
wrt.
X^{l}\in\mathsf{Occ}(\mathcal{K})
.
The constraint

\displaystyle\mathrm{at\_most}(u,\mathsf{At}_{\mathrm{forget}})

(SF4)

is added to
S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K},u)
in order to keep the number of forgotten occurrences from exceeding
u
.
In total,
S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is comprised of (SF1)–(SF4) (see Encoding
2
for a complete overview).
The proof for the following theorem, along with Encoding
2
, is provided in Appendix
A.1.2

Theorem 2
.

For a given value
u
, the encoding
S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is satisfiable if and only if
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K})\leq u
.

Example 11
.

Consider again the knowledge base
\mathcal{K}_{7}
from Example
10
:

\mathcal{K}_{7}=\left\{\underbrace{\overbrace{x}^{\phi_{1,1}}\land\overbrace{y}^{\phi_{1,2}}}_{\alpha_{1}},\quad\underbrace{\overbrace{x}^{\phi_{2,1}}\lor\overbrace{y}^{\phi_{2,2}}}_{\alpha_{2}},\quad\underbrace{\lnot\overbrace{x}^{\phi_{3}}}_{\alpha_{3}}\right\}

In this example, we aim to construct
S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K}_{7},2)
, i. e., a SAT encoding which returns true if and only if
2
is an upper bound of the forgetting-based inconsistency value of
\mathcal{K}_{7}
.

To begin with, we need
|\mathsf{Occ}(\mathcal{K}_{7})|\cdot 2=5\cdot 2=10
atoms that represent the forgetting operation wrt. each atom occurrence (SF1):

t_{x,1},f_{x,1},t_{y,1},f_{y,1},\qquad t_{x,2},f_{x,2},t_{y,2},f_{y,2},\qquad t_{x,3},f_{x,3}

We substitute each occurrence in every formula in
\mathcal{K}_{7}
as described by (SF2).
This produces the following
3
formulas, which are all added to
S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K}_{7},2)
:

\displaystyle((t_{x,1}\lor x)\land\neg f_{x,1})\land((t_{y,1}\lor y)\land\neg f_{y,1})

\displaystyle((t_{x,2}\lor x)\land\neg f_{x,2})\lor((t_{y,2}\lor y)\land\neg f_{y,2})

\displaystyle\lnot((t_{x,3}\lor x)\land\neg f_{x,3})

Next, we apply (SF3):

\displaystyle\lnot t_{x,1}\lor\lnot f_{x,1}

\displaystyle\qquad\lnot t_{y,1}\lor\lnot f_{y,1}

\displaystyle\lnot t_{x,2}\lor\lnot f_{x,2}

\displaystyle\qquad\lnot t_{y,2}\lor\lnot f_{y,2}

\displaystyle\lnot t_{x,3}\lor\lnot f_{x,3}

Finally, we add the at-most-
u
constraint (SF4) to
S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K}_{7},2)
:

\mathrm{at\_most}(2,\{t_{x,1},f_{x,1},t_{y,1},f_{y,1},t_{x,2},f_{x,2},t_{y,2},f_{y,2},t_{x,3},f_{x,3}\})

3.5
The Hitting Set Inconsistency Measure

In the following, we propose a SAT encoding, denoted
S_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathcal{K},u)
, for the problem
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}(\mathcal{K})
, with
\mathcal{K}
being a knowledge base and
u
an integer representing a candidate for an upper bound.
We reiterate that
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}(\mathcal{K})
is the minimum cardinality of a set of interpretations such that there is at least one model for every formula in the knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
, subtracted by
1
.
This is equivalent to finding the minimal number of blocks of a partitioning of
\mathcal{K}
such that each block is satisfiable (again, subtracted by
1
).

The formulas in
\mathcal{K}
are partitioned into
u
blocks, and every formula belongs to at least one block.
For every formula
A\in\mathcal{K}
, we create copies
A_{i}
with
i=1,\ldots,u
.
The meaning of formula
A_{i}
is “the
i
-th copy of formula
A
”.
Then, we create
\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert\cdot u
new atoms
p_{A,i}
(SH2).
The meaning of the atom
p_{A,i}
is “formula
A
is a member of block
i
”.
For every
i
, all atoms
X
that appear in a formula
A_{i}
are replaced with new atoms
X_{i}
(SH1), meaning each block has its own copy of
\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
, allowing us to check for satisfiability block-wise.

For every block, the set of formulas inside it is satisfiable.
We add
\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert\cdot u
constraints of the form

\displaystyle p_{A,i}\rightarrow A_{i}.

(SH3)

This models that if a formula
A
belongs to block
i
, then the corresponding formula
A_{i}
must be satisfied (within this block).
Every formula must be sorted into at least one block, meaning for all
A\in\mathcal{K}
, at least one
p_{A,i}
is true.
A simple encoding for the “at-least-one” constraint is to add the following clauses for every
A\in\mathcal{K}
:

\displaystyle\bigvee_{1\leq i\leq u}p_{A,i}

(SH4)

When a value for the minimal number of satisfiable partition blocks is found, we subtract
1
to generate the value
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}(\mathcal{K})
. If no value is found in the range, the result is
\infty
.

The following result establishes that the above encoding
S_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathcal{K},u)
, defined by (SH1)–(SH4) (for an overview, see Encoding
3
in Appendix
A.1.3
), faithfully implements
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}
.
The proof of the theorem below is given in Appendix
A.1.3
as well.

Theorem 3
.

For a given value
u
, the encoding
S_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is satisfiable if and only if
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}(\mathcal{K})\leq u-1
.

S_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is unsatisfiable for all
u=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{K}|
if and only if
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}(\mathcal{K})=\infty
.

Example 12
.

Consider again the knowledge base
\mathcal{K}_{7}
from Example
10
:

\mathcal{K}_{7}=\left\{\underbrace{\overbrace{x}^{\phi_{1,1}}\land\overbrace{y}^{\phi_{1,2}}}_{\alpha_{1}},\quad\underbrace{\overbrace{x}^{\phi_{2,1}}\lor\overbrace{y}^{\phi_{2,2}}}_{\alpha_{2}},\quad\underbrace{\lnot\overbrace{x}^{\phi_{3}}}_{\alpha_{3}}\right\}

In this example, we aim to construct
S_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathcal{K}_{7},2)
, i. e., a SAT encoding which returns true if and only if
3-1=2
is an upper bound of the hitting set inconsistency value of
\mathcal{K}_{7}
.

First, we create variables for each atom
x_{i}\in\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K}_{7})=\{x,y\}
with
i\in\{1,2\}
(SH1):

x_{1},x_{2},\qquad y_{1},y_{2}

In addition, for each formula in
\mathcal{K}_{7}
we create the following variables (SH2):

p_{\alpha_{1},1},p_{\alpha_{1},2},\qquad p_{\alpha_{2},1},p_{\alpha_{2},2},\qquad p_{\alpha_{3},1},p_{\alpha_{3},2},

Now we create indexed copies of the formulas in
\mathcal{K}_{7}
:

\displaystyle x_{1}\land y_{1},

\displaystyle\qquad x_{1}\lor y_{1},

\displaystyle\qquad\lnot x_{1},

\displaystyle x_{2}\land y_{2},

\displaystyle\qquad x_{2}\lor y_{2},

\displaystyle\qquad\lnot x_{2}

Following (SH3), we add the subsequent formulas to
S_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathcal{K}_{7},2)
:

\displaystyle p_{\alpha_{1},1}\rightarrow(x_{1}\land y_{1}),

\displaystyle\qquad p_{\alpha_{2},1}\rightarrow(x_{1}\lor y_{1}),

\displaystyle\qquad p_{\alpha_{3},1}\rightarrow(\lnot x_{1}),

\displaystyle p_{\alpha_{1},2}\rightarrow(x_{2}\land y_{2}),

\displaystyle\qquad p_{\alpha_{2},2}\rightarrow(x_{2}\lor y_{2}),

\displaystyle\qquad p_{\alpha_{3},2}\rightarrow(\lnot x_{2})

At last, we add the following constraints via (SH4):

\displaystyle p_{\alpha_{1},1}\lor p_{\alpha_{1},2},\qquad p_{\alpha_{2},1}\lor p_{\alpha_{2},2},\qquad p_{\alpha_{3},1}\lor p_{\alpha_{3},2}

3.6
The Max-Distance Inconsistency Measure

Let, once again,
\mathcal{K}
be a propositional knowledge base, and let
u
be an integer representing a potential upper bound for
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K})
.
In the following, we construct a SAT encoding
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K},u)
for the problem
\textsc{upper}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}}
.

As a reminder, the distance-based inconsistency measures look for an interpretation with an “optimal” distance. Regarding
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}
, the “optimal” interpretation has the minimal maximum distance to the models of all formulas in
\mathcal{K}
.
In case one or more formulas in
\mathcal{K}
are unsatisfiable, i. e., they possess no models,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K})=\infty
.

First we need a representation of the “optimal” interpretation that will be computed by the measure.
For each atom
X\in\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
, we add a new atom
X_{o}
that represents this atom’s value assignment in the “optimal interpretation” (SDM1). Let
\mathsf{At}_{O}
be the set of atoms of the optimal interpretation, i. e.,
\mathsf{At}_{O}=\{X_{o}\mid X\in\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})\}
.

With the exception of the case that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K})=\infty
(i. e., the case in which at least one of the formulas is contradictory), all formulas in
\mathcal{K}
must be satisfied by some interpretation.
For every formula
A\in\mathcal{K}
, we create a clone
A_{i}
with
i\in\{1,\ldots,\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert\}
.
For every
i
, all atoms
X
that appear in a formula
A_{i}
are replaced with new atoms
X_{i}
(SDM2).
We add the modified clones
A_{i}
to the encoding (SDM4).
This models that the formulas are all satisfiable individually and allows us to put their models and the optimal interpretation in relation in the next step.

For each formula, the distance between at least one model of the formula and the optimal interpretation consists of at most
u
differing atom assignments.
We now aim to model that the minimum distance between some model of each formula and the optimal world is at most
u
.
This can also be formulated as “for each formula, there is some model that can be converted into the optimal world by inverting at most
u
value assignments” (excluding the
\infty
case).
To represent this, we create new atoms
\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}
with
i\in\{1,\ldots,\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert\}
for all
X_{i}\in\mathsf{At}(S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K},u))\setminus\mathsf{At}_{O}
that model the inverted value assignments (SDM3).
Then, we add the following formulas for every
X_{i}
, using the optimal interpretation atoms
X_{o}\in\mathsf{At}_{O}
:

\displaystyle X_{i}

\displaystyle\rightarrow X_{o}\lor\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}

(SDM5)

\displaystyle\neg X_{i}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\lnot X_{o}\lor\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}

(SDM6)

The above formulas represent that for every atom
X_{i}
of a model of a formula, the corresponding atom in the optimal interpretation has the same value unless it is inverted by setting
\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}
to true.
We then represent the upper bound by adding at-most-
u
constraints for all
\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}
. Let
\mathsf{INV}_{i}
denote the set containing all
\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}
for a fixed
i
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{at\_most}(u,\mathsf{INV}_{i})

(SDM7)

In total, the SAT encoding
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K},u)
comprises (SDM1)–(SDM7). The following result establishes that the above encoding faithfully implements
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}
.

Theorem 4
.

For a given value
u
, the encoding
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K},u)
is satisfiable if and only if
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K})\leq u
.

S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K},u)
is unsatisfiable for all
u\in\{0,\ldots,\lvert\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})\rvert\}
if and only if
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K})=\infty
.

The proof of the above theorem, as well as Encoding
4
, which contains a complete overview of
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K},u)
, are provided in Appendix
A.1.4
.

Example 13
.

Consider again the knowledge base
\mathcal{K}_{7}
from Example
10
:

\mathcal{K}_{7}=\left\{\underbrace{\overbrace{x}^{\phi_{1,1}}\land\overbrace{y}^{\phi_{1,2}}}_{\alpha_{1}},\quad\underbrace{\overbrace{x}^{\phi_{2,1}}\lor\overbrace{y}^{\phi_{2,2}}}_{\alpha_{2}},\quad\underbrace{\lnot\overbrace{x}^{\phi_{3}}}_{\alpha_{3}}\right\}

In this example, we aim to construct
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K}_{7},1)
, i. e., a SAT encoding which returns true if and only if
1
is an upper bound of the max-distance inconsistency value of
\mathcal{K}_{7}
.

At first, we create the variables
x_{o},y_{o}
for the atoms in
\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K}_{7})=\{x,y\}
(SDM1).
We create additional variables for each atom in
\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K}_{7})
wrt. indices
i\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathcal{K}_{7}|\}
with
|\mathcal{K}_{7}|=3
via (SDM2) and (SDM3):

\displaystyle x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},

\displaystyle\qquad y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}

\displaystyle\mathsf{inv}_{x,1},\mathsf{inv}_{x,2},\mathsf{inv}_{x,3},

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{inv}_{y,1},\mathsf{inv}_{y,2},\mathsf{inv}_{y,3}

We add indexed copies of the formulas in
\mathcal{K}_{7}
to
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K}_{7},1)
(SDM4):

\displaystyle x_{1}\land y_{1},

\displaystyle\qquad x_{2}\lor y_{2},

\displaystyle\qquad\lnot x_{3}

We now add the following constraints conforming to (SDM5) and (SDM6) to
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K}_{7},1)
:

\displaystyle x_{1}\rightarrow x_{o}\lor\mathsf{inv}_{x,1},

\displaystyle\qquad\lnot x_{1}\rightarrow\lnot x_{o}\lor\mathsf{inv}_{x,1},

\displaystyle y_{1}\rightarrow y_{o}\lor\mathsf{inv}_{y,1},

\displaystyle\qquad\lnot y_{1}\rightarrow\lnot y_{o}\lor\mathsf{inv}_{y,1},

\displaystyle x_{2}\rightarrow x_{o}\lor\mathsf{inv}_{x,2},

\displaystyle\qquad\lnot x_{2}\rightarrow\lnot x_{o}\lor\mathsf{inv}_{x,2},

\displaystyle y_{2}\rightarrow y_{o}\lor\mathsf{inv}_{y,2},

\displaystyle\qquad\lnot y_{2}\rightarrow\lnot y_{o}\lor\mathsf{inv}_{y,2},

\displaystyle x_{3}\rightarrow x_{o}\lor\mathsf{inv}_{x,3},

\displaystyle\qquad\lnot x_{3}\rightarrow\lnot x_{o}\lor\mathsf{inv}_{x,3},

\displaystyle y_{3}\rightarrow y_{o}\lor\mathsf{inv}_{y,3},

\displaystyle\qquad\lnot y_{3}\rightarrow\lnot y_{o}\lor\mathsf{inv}_{y,3}

Lastly, the following at-most-
u
constraints are added:

\displaystyle\mathrm{at\_most}(1,\{\mathsf{inv}_{x,1},\mathsf{inv}_{y,1}\}),\quad\mathrm{at\_most}(1,\{\mathsf{inv}_{x,2},\mathsf{inv}_{y,2}\}),\quad\mathrm{at\_most}(1,\{\mathsf{inv}_{x,3},\mathsf{inv}_{y,3}\})

3.7
The Sum-Distance Inconsistency Measure

The definition of the sum-distance measure
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}
is quite similar to the definition of the previously addressed max-distance measure
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}
.
The only difference is the notion of optimality—while
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}
seeks the interpretation with the smallest maximum distance to the models of the formulas in a given knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}
seeks an interpretation such that the sum of its distances to the models of the formulas in
\mathcal{K}
is minimal.

Let once again
u
be an integer representing a candidate for an upper bound of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K})
.
In the following, we construct a SAT encoding
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K},u)
for the problem
\textsc{upper}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}}
.

The constraints (SDS1)–(SDS6) of the encoding
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K},u)
are identical to constraints (SDM1)–(SDM6) of
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K},u)
.
Only the use of the at-most-
u
constraints is changed—we represent the upper bound for the sum of the distances by adding a single at-most-
u
constraint for all
\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}
with
i\in\{1,\ldots,\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert\}
.
Let
\mathsf{INV}
be the set containing all
\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{at\_most}(u,\textsf{INV})

(SDS7)

Altogether,
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K},u)
is comprised of (SDS1)–(SDS7) (for an overview, see Encoding
5
in Appendix
A.1.5
).
The following result establishes that the above encoding faithfully implements
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}
.
The corresponding proof can likewise be found in Appendix
A.1.5
.

Theorem 5
.

For a given value
u
, the encoding
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K},u)
is satisfiable if and only if
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K})\leq u
.

S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K},u)
is unsatisfiable for all
u\in\{0,\ldots,\lvert\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})\rvert\cdot\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert\}
if and only if
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K})=\infty
.

Example 14
.

Consider again the knowledge base
\mathcal{K}_{7}
from Example
10
:

\mathcal{K}_{7}=\left\{\underbrace{\overbrace{x}^{\phi_{1,1}}\land\overbrace{y}^{\phi_{1,2}}}_{\alpha_{1}},\quad\underbrace{\overbrace{x}^{\phi_{2,1}}\lor\overbrace{y}^{\phi_{2,2}}}_{\alpha_{2}},\quad\underbrace{\lnot\overbrace{x}^{\phi_{3}}}_{\alpha_{3}}\right\}

In this example, we aim to construct
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K}_{7},2)
, i. e., a SAT encoding which returns true if and only if
2
is an upper bound of the sum-distance inconsistency value of
\mathcal{K}_{7}
.

The variables we require (defined by (SDS1)–(SDS3)) are the same as those in Example
13
. Furthermore, the constraints we need to add to
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K}_{7},2)
via (SDS4)–(SDS6) correspond exactly to those defined by (SDM4)–(SDM6) in Example
13
.
However, we use a different at-most-
u
constraint following (SDS7):

\mathrm{at\_most}(2,\{\mathsf{inv}_{x,1},\mathsf{inv}_{y,1},\mathsf{inv}_{x,2},\mathsf{inv}_{y,2},\mathsf{inv}_{x,3},\mathsf{inv}_{y,3}\})

3.8
The Hit-Distance Inconsistency Measure

Let
\mathcal{K}
once again be a knowledge base and
u
an integer representing a possible upper bound of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\text{hit}}(\mathcal{K})
.
In the following, we construct a SAT encoding
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\mathrm{hit}}(\mathcal{K},u)
for
\textsc{upper}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\text{hit}}}
.

In contrast to the other distance-based measures, the value of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\text{hit}}(\mathcal{K})
is not a distance value, but a count of distances greater than
0
.
In other words,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\text{hit}}(\mathcal{K})
is the maximum number of formulas in
\mathcal{K}
that can be satisfied by one interpretation, or the minimum number of formulas that need to be removed in order to make
\mathcal{K}
consistent.
To represent this, we add one new variable
\mathsf{hit}_{A}
for every formula
A\in\mathcal{K}
(SDH1).
Let
\mathsf{HIT_{\mathcal{K}}}=\{\mathsf{hit}_{A}\mid A\in\mathcal{K}\}
.
For each
A\in\mathcal{K}
, we also add a disjunction that uses one of the new variables:

\displaystyle A\vee\mathsf{hit}_{A}

(SDH3)

This formula represents that if
A
is not true, then the new variable
\mathsf{hit}_{A}
is set to true.
Note that all atoms of the input knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
still appear in the encoding as part of
A
(SDH2).
We then represent the upper bound for the number of models with distances
>0
by adding one at-most-
u
constraint that restricts the number of
\mathsf{hit}_{A}\in\mathsf{HIT_{\mathcal{K}}}
that are allowed to be set to true:

\displaystyle\mathrm{at\_most}(u,\mathsf{HIT_{\mathcal{K}}})

(SDH4)

In total,
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\mathrm{hit}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is defined by (SDH1)–(SDH4). Appendix
A.1.6
provides an overview of
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\mathrm{hit}}(\mathcal{K},u)
(i. e., Encoding
6
), as well as the correctness proof of the theorem below.

Theorem 6
.

For a given value
u
, the encoding
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\mathrm{hit}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is satisfiable if and only if
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\text{hit}}(\mathcal{K})\leq u
.

Example 15
.

Consider again the knowledge base
\mathcal{K}_{7}
from Example
10
:

\mathcal{K}_{7}=\left\{\underbrace{\overbrace{x}^{\phi_{1,1}}\land\overbrace{y}^{\phi_{1,2}}}_{\alpha_{1}},\quad\underbrace{\overbrace{x}^{\phi_{2,1}}\lor\overbrace{y}^{\phi_{2,2}}}_{\alpha_{2}},\quad\underbrace{\lnot\overbrace{x}^{\phi_{3}}}_{\alpha_{3}}\right\}

In this example, we aim to construct
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\mathrm{hit}}(\mathcal{K}_{7},1)
, i. e., a SAT encoding which returns true if and only if
1
is an upper bound of the hit-distance inconsistency value of
\mathcal{K}_{7}
.

First, following (SDH1), we define the variables
\mathsf{hit}_{\alpha_{1}},\mathsf{hit}_{\alpha_{2}},\mathsf{hit}_{\alpha_{3}}
wrt. the formulas in
\mathcal{K}_{7}
.
Moreover, we require the variables
\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K}_{7})=\{x,y\}
(SDH2).
Following (SDH3), we add the subsequent constraints to
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\mathrm{hit}}(\mathcal{K}_{7},1)
:

\displaystyle(x\land y)\lor\mathsf{hit}_{\alpha_{1}},\qquad(x\lor y)\lor\mathsf{hit}_{\alpha_{2}},\qquad(\lnot x)\lor\mathsf{hit}_{\alpha_{3}}

At last, we add
\mathrm{at\_most}(1,\{\mathsf{hit}_{\alpha_{1}},\mathsf{hit}_{\alpha_{2}},\mathsf{hit}_{\alpha_{3}}\})
via (SDH4).

4
ASP-Based Algorithms for Selected Inconsistency Measures

After the introduction of a general SAT-based approach for computing inconsistency degrees, as well as specific encodings for the problem
\textsc{upper}_{\mathcal{I}}
wrt. all six inconsistency measures addressed in this work, we now introduce answer set programming as an alternative method to compute inconsistency values.

4.1
Answer Set Programming

Answer Set Programming
(ASP) (?, ?, ?, ?) is a declarative problem solving approach targeted at difficult search problems.
Thus, rather than modeling instructions on how to solve a problem, a representation of the problem itself is modeled.
The goal is to represent a problem in a logical format (a
logic program
) such that the models of this representation describe solutions of the original problem.
These models are called
answer sets
.

In short, a logic program is a finite set of
rules
of the form

\displaystyle r=a_{0}\texttt{ :- }a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},\texttt{ not }a_{n+1},\ldots,\texttt{ not }a_{m}.

with each
a_{i}
(
0\leq i\leq n\leq m
) being atoms, and “
not
” denoting the default negation in the sense of Reiter (?).
In ASP, an atom has the form
p(\mathsf{t}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{t}_{n})
, with
p
being a predicate symbol, and
\mathsf{t}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{t}_{n}
being terms.
Terms are either constants, variables, arithmetic terms (i. e.,
-\mathsf{t}_{1}
or
\mathsf{t}_{1}\circ\mathsf{t}_{2}
with
\circ\in\{+,-,*,/\}
wrt. some terms
\mathsf{t}_{1},\mathsf{t}_{2}
), or functional terms (i. e.,
\varphi(\mathsf{t}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{t}_{m})
with
\varphi
being a functor,
\mathsf{t}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{t}_{m}
being terms, and
m>0
) (?).
Moreover, we express the arity
n
of a predicate or function
\varphi
as
\varphi/n
, and an ASP literal
\ell
is either an atom or a default-negated atom.

The
head
of a rule
r
(as shown above) is
\mathsf{head}(r)=\{a_{0}\}

and the
body
is
\mathsf{body}(r)=\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},\texttt{not}\,a_{n+1},\ldots,\texttt{not}\,a_{m}\}
.
If
\mathsf{body}(r)=\emptyset
, then
r
is a
fact
, and
if
\mathsf{head}(r)=\emptyset
, then
r
is a
constraint
.
We further divide the body elements of
r
into
\mathsf{body}^{+}(r)=\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}
and
\mathsf{body}^{-}(r)=\{a_{n+1},\ldots,a_{m}\}
.

An atom/rule/program is
ground
if it does not contain any variables.
3
3

3

Note that, following the Clingo syntax, all variable names we use start with an uppercase letter, and all constant names start with a lowercase letter.
We also make use of
anonymous variables
. Those are variables that do not recur within a rule, and that are denoted by “
_
”.

Let
X
be a set of ground atoms.
We define
X
to be a
model
of a ground logic program
P
if for all
r\in P
,
\mathsf{head}(r)\in X
whenever
\mathsf{body}^{+}(r)\subseteq X
and
\mathsf{body}^{-}(r)\cap X=\emptyset
.
The
reduct
(?) of
P
wrt.
X
is defined as

P^{X}=\{\mathsf{head}(r)\texttt{ :- }\mathsf{body}^{+}(r)\mid\mathsf{body}^{-}(r)\cap X=\emptyset\text{ with }r\in P\}

Moreover,
X
is an
answer set
of
P
if it is a subset-minimal model of
P^{X}
.

Another language concept we make use of is the
conditional literal
, which is of the form
\ell_{0}:\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}
, with
\ell_{0},\ldots,\ell_{n}
being literals.
The idea behind a conditional literal is to regulate the instantiation of
\ell_{0}
by means of
\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}
.
In other words, we can view a conditional literal as the set
\{\ell_{0}\mid\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}\}
.
Moreover, we use
cardinality constraints
.
A cardinality constraint is of the form
l\{c_{1};\ldots;c_{m}\}u
, with
c_{1},\ldots,c_{m}
being conditional literals,
l
constituting an optional lower bound, and
u
an optional upper bound.
