5.3.5
Core-Guided Optimization in ASP

Modern ASP systems, such as Clingo, allow for the use of different optimization strategies.
Clingo’s default option (which is used in all experiments described above) is a
branch-and-bound
approach (?).
In this approach, we first search for an initial solution, i. e., an initial stable model, and (in case a solution exists) obtain its objective value.
The next step consists of a loop in which we try to find a solution with a strictly “better” objective value—i. e., in the case of minimization, a strictly lower value, and in the case of maximization, a strictly higher value.
This is achieved by adding constraints which ensure that any new solution must have a lower (resp. higher) objective value than the solution derived before.
If no “better” solution can be found, we know that the previous solution corresponds to an optimum (?).

Although the branch-and-bound optimization approach led to promising results in our previous experiments, Clingo has further optimization techniques to offer, which we have yet to explore.
As an example, we perform an experiment in which we set Clingo’s optimization strategy to “
usc
” (?), employs a
core-guided
approach (?).
The latter emerged in the area of MaxSAT solving (?, ?) and is based on the following procedure.
We first check if all soft clauses can be satisfied, i. e., we implicitly introduce a lower bound of
0
soft clauses that are allowed to be falsified.
If this is not the case, we allow for at most one soft clause to be falsified (i. e., we increase the lower bound to
1
), and check for satisfiability again.
This process is repeated until we find a satisfiable solution.
In order to guide this process, at each step an
unsatisfiable core
(an unsatisfiable set of clauses) is extracted and only soft clauses from that a core are allowed to be falsified (?).
This approach has been transferred to ASP (?), with the literals of ASP optimization statements being interpreted as the soft clauses of a MaxSAT problem.

Since, in the case of minimization, core-guided optimization is useful when the target values are close to
0
, we selected inconsistency measures and data sets for which this applies (see the histograms in Appendix
B.2
).
To be precise, we performed an experiment with the ASP approach for
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}
on the ML data set; here, the inconsistency values tend to be very low (either
0
or
1
; see Figure
13
), while the search space (i. e., the number of formulas) is still quite large (the mean number of formulas is
7506
; see Table
4
).
In addition, we considered the ARG data set in combination with
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}
, as the inconsistency values are likewise always
0
or
1
.

5.3.6
Previous ASP Approaches

There already exist two ASP-based approaches for
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{c}}}
, and one for
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{f}}
, and
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}
, respectively, in the literature.
To be precise, in (?), the authors propose a method similar to our overall SAT approach, which uses ASP encodings for the problem
\textsc{upper}_{\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{c}}}}
in order to find
\textsc{value}_{\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{c}}}}
via binary search.
A revised version of this approach, which calculates
\textsc{value}_{\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{c}}}}
directly within ASP by means of a minimize statement, is introduced in (?).
Note that in the latter version, only propositional language concepts are used, which leads to a program that is already ground. The authors also propose ASP encodings for
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{f}}
and
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}
in the same manner.
In contrast, the ASP approach presented in (?) (which is also used in the work at hand) makes use of first-order predicates and variables, which enables an automated, and internally optimized, grounding procedure.
In order to determine whether this actually has a positive effect on the runtime, we compare the different versions with each other using the contension measure as an example.
Note that we apply exactly those implementations which were used in the two corresponding papers (?, ?).

5.4
Discussion

In the experiments presented above, we investigated three approaches for the calculation of six different inconsistency measures on five different data sets.
Overall, we focused our analysis on the comparison of runtimes; however, we took additional aspects into account, such as the distribution of measured inconsistency values with regard to the different measures and data sets.
As a first step, we compared the runtimes of all three approaches per inconsistency measure and data set.
Our results confirmed that both the SAT-based and the ASP-based approaches perform, as expected, superior to the naive baseline algorithms.
Moreover, the results showed that altogether, the ASP methods are faster than the SAT variants.
Only in very isolated cases can the SAT-based or naive approaches retrieve inconsistency values faster than the ASP-based ones or result in fewer timeouts.
Furthermore, the ASP-based methods are not only faster than the other two approaches on average, but also wrt. the vast majority of individual instances.

Two factors that presumably play a role in the performance differences between the SAT approaches and the ASP approaches are the following.
First, in each SAT-based approach, we need to generate a new at-most-
k
constraint in each iteration (note that this has been addressed in Section
5.3.2
as well).
Second, the SAT solver does not reuse learned clauses from previous calls, i. e., each instance has to be constructed from scratch.
The ASP encodings, on the other hand, only have to be constructed and solved once for each knowledge base.

We could further observe that the naive baseline methods for the contension and the forgetting-based measure outperformed the ASP-based and SAT-based approaches in some cases. The most likely reason for this is the fact that the particular instances for which this effect was observed had a very low inconsistency value—in fact, most of these instances were consistent.
The baseline methods for both the contension measure and the forgetting-based measure include a satisfiability check (via a SAT solver) as a first step.
Hence, consistent knowledge bases can be identified rather quickly.
Very small inconsistency values might also be retrieved rather fast by the two naive methods, as they both search for the correct value in a linear manner, starting from the lowest possible value. Another factor that can further decrease the runtime of the two methods is if the given knowledge base is already in CNF, so that no additional transformation steps are required in order to guarantee a valid input for the SAT solver.

A general observation from the evaluation results is that none of the three approaches for either one of the considered inconsistency measures could solve all instances from all data sets.
Hence, even the ASP approaches, which altogether performed superior, clearly reached their limits.
In the following, we aim to identify factors that make the computation of inconsistency values for a given knowledge base “difficult” (either for individual approaches or in general).

•

A rather obvious factor is the size of the given knowledge base in terms of the number of formulas, number of atoms in the signature, and number of connectives per formula (see Table
4
for an overview regarding the data sets used in our experiments).
A larger knowledge base consequently leads to larger encodings, as well as a larger range of possible inconsistency values, which in turn leads to a more difficult problem that the SAT or ASP solver has to solve, as well as to more search steps wrt. the SAT-based approaches.
The SAT/ASP solver having to deal with a more difficult problem is directly connected to an increase in runtime, as the results in Section
5.3.2
indicated that the solving process overall makes up the largest fraction of the runtimes of both the SAT-based and ASP-based methods.

•

Although all six measures considered in this paper lie on the same level of the polynomial hierarchy (?), the ranges of their possible values vary greatly.
A larger search space particularly influences the SAT-based approaches.
This is reflected, e.g., in the fact that for the sum-distance measure, fewer instances could be solved by the SAT-based approach than for any of the other measures.

•

The range of the resulting inconsistency values may also be a relevant factor to take into account.
If the inconsistency values are close to
0
, a SAT check as a preprocessing step could prove useful—we observed this effect with the naive approaches for the contension measure and the forgetting-based measure, in particular if the given knowledge base is already in CNF.
Moreover, the range of the inconsistency values is also relevant for the choice of search strategy regarding the SAT-based approaches (see Section
5.3.3
).

Although the previously discussed points hint at which measures might be more suitable in practice in terms of runtime, the choice of an appropriate inconsistency measure for a specific practical application still depends on other factors as well.
For instance, in some scenarios it could be useful to look for conflicting formulas (which could be done by using the hitting set measure or the hit-distance measure), while in other scenarios it could be of greater interest to look for the atoms involved in a conflict (which could be done by using the contension or forgetting-based measure).
Moreover, the granularity of the measured inconsistency could be of interest (e. g., the forgetting-based measure has a finer granularity than the contension measure).
Yet another aspect that should be taken into consideration when dealing with a practical application is the usefulness of the inconsistency measure for resolving the conflicts in the given knowledge base.

6
Conclusion

In the course of this work, we proposed a SAT-based and an ASP-based approach for each of six different inconsistency measures (i. e., the contension, the forgetting-based, the hitting set, the max-distance, the sum-distance, and the hit-distance inconsistency measure).
With the SAT-based approaches, we encode the problem of whether a given value is an upper bound of the inconsistency degree, and retrieve the actual inconsistency value by means of iterative SAT solver calls in a (binary) search procedure.
In ASP, we can encode the problem of finding an inconsistency value directly by utilizing optimization statements.

In an extensive experimental evaluation we compared the ASP-based to the SAT-based approaches, focusing on runtime.
To achieve this, we used a total of five different data sets, two of which were used in preceding works (?, ?), while the remaining three are novel.
All data sets are publicly available for use in future work.
The results of our experiments first of all demonstrated that both the SAT-based and the ASP-based methods overall clearly outperform the naive implementations we used as a baseline—which, to the best of our knowledge, are the only previously existing implementations for the inconsistency measures considered.
Moreover, the ASP approaches performed superior to the SAT approaches (with only very few exceptions).

Nonetheless, the results also showed that even the ASP methods are by far not able to solve all instances from all data sets wrt. all considered inconsistency measures.
Some data sets turned out to be generally challenging; an example of this is the SC data set, which contains benchmark data from the SAT competition 2020.
A reason for this lies in the sheer size of the individual knowledge bases, i. e., the number of formulas contained in a knowledge base, its signature size, and the number of connectives per formula.
A large knowledge base requires a large SAT/ASP encoding (in terms of variables and clauses/rules), which in turn leads to an increased solving time and therefore an increased runtime—our results also showed that the solving process altogether makes up the largest fraction of the runtime composition of both the SAT and the ASP approaches.

In order to use approaches to inconsistency measurement in a practical application in a sensible fashion, an analysis of the given data is vital.
Information about signature sizes or numbers of formulas per knowledge base can be an indicator for which inconsistency measure will likely be a reasonable choice in terms of runtime.
Moreover, if there is any information available about what degrees of inconsistency can roughly be expected, an adjusted search strategy can be used.
Such information can further be used to develop preprocessing techniques.
For instance, if there is a certain probability that the knowledge bases at hand might be consistent, an initial satisfiability check by means of a SAT solver (as we saw with the naive implementations of the contension and the forgetting-based measure) could prove useful.
Certainly there are many other options regarding the topic of preprocessing; however, this is subject to future work.

Another issue to contemplate in terms of future work is a more in-depth examination of MaxSAT approaches.
We have already observed in our experiments that a MaxSAT approach for the contension inconsistency measure performs superior to its iterative SAT counterpart.
Moreover, there are numerous problem solving paradigms that have not been explored yet in the field of inconsistency measurement.
Examples include
Integer Linear Programming
,
Quantified Boolean Formulas
, and
Counterexample-Guided Abstraction Refinement
approaches.
The choice of problem solving paradigm depends on the complexity of the measure at hand—which is related to yet another possible topic of future work: the investigation of algorithms for inconistency measures beyond the first level of the polynomial hierarchy.
Furthermore, we merely covered inconsistency measures designed for propositional knowledge bases.
The development of approaches for computing inconsistencies in other domains, such as different logics, data bases, or business process models, constitutes another open problem.
In addition, our evaluation showed that all approaches introduced in this work hit their limit at some point during the experiments.
To counteract this effect, i. e., to develop more scalable approaches, we already mentioned the possibility of applying preprocessing methods.
A different strategy to improve scalability could be to apply approximation techniques.

The research reported here was supported by the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft under grant 506604007.

Appendix A
Proofs

This appendix contains the correctness proofs for the SAT-based approaches introduced in Section
3
, and the ASP-based approaches introduced in Section
4
.

A.1
SAT-based Algorithms

Each of the following sections comprises a correctness proof corresponding to the SAT encoding for one of the six inconsistency measures considered in this work.

A.1.1
The Contension Inconsistency Measure

Encoding 1:
Overview of the SAT encoding
S_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{K},u)
of the contension inconsistency measure
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{c}}}
.

\mathcal{K}
is a given knowledge base and
\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
its signature.

u
is a candidate for an upper limit of
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{c}}}(\mathcal{K})
.
Let
\mathsf{sub}(\mathcal{K})
be the set of subformulas of
\mathcal{K}
as defined in Section
3.1
.
The signature size of the encoding is
\lvert\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})\rvert\cdot 3+\lvert\mathsf{sub}(\mathcal{K})\rvert\cdot 3
.

Signature

For every atom
X\in\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
:

Create atoms
X_{t}
,
X_{f}
,
X_{b}

(SC1)

For every formula
\Phi\in\mathsf{sub}(\mathcal{K})
:

Create atoms
v^{t}_{\Phi}
,
v^{f}_{\Phi}
,
v^{b}_{\Phi}

(SC2)

Constraints

For every atom
X\in\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
:

(X_{t}\vee X_{f}\vee X_{b})\wedge(\neg X_{t}\vee\neg X_{f})\wedge(\neg X_{t}\vee\neg X_{b})\wedge(\neg X_{b}\vee\neg X_{f})

(SC3)

For every conjunction
\Phi_{c}=\Psi_{c,1}\land\Psi_{c,2}
appearing in some formula:

v_{\Phi_{c}}^{t}\leftrightarrow v_{\Psi_{c,1}}^{t}\wedge v_{\Psi_{c,2}}^{t}

(SC4)

v_{\Phi_{c}}^{f}\leftrightarrow v_{\Psi_{c,1}}^{f}\vee v_{\Psi_{c,2}}^{f}

(SC5)

v_{\Phi_{c}}^{b}\leftrightarrow(\neg v_{\Psi_{c,1}}^{t}\vee\neg v_{\Psi_{c,2}}^{t})\wedge\neg v_{\Psi_{c,1}}^{f}\wedge\neg v_{\Psi_{c,2}}^{f}

(SC6)

For every disjunction
\Phi_{d}=\Psi_{d,1}\lor\Psi_{d,2}
appearing in some formula:

v_{\Phi_{d}}^{t}\leftrightarrow v_{\Psi_{d,1}}^{t}\vee v_{\Psi_{d,2}}^{t}

(SC7)

v_{\Phi_{d}}^{f}\leftrightarrow v_{\Psi_{d,1}}^{f}\wedge v_{\Psi_{d,2}}^{f}

(SC8)

v_{\Phi_{d}}^{b}\leftrightarrow(\neg v_{\Psi_{d,1}}^{f}\vee\neg v_{\Psi_{d,2}}^{f})\wedge\neg v_{\Psi_{d,1}}^{t}\wedge\neg v_{\Psi_{d,2}}^{t}

(SC9)

For each negation
\Phi_{n}=\lnot\Psi_{n}
appearing in some formula:

v^{t}_{\Phi_{n}}\leftrightarrow v^{f}_{\Psi_{n}}

(SC10)

v^{f}_{\Phi_{n}}\leftrightarrow v^{t}_{\Psi_{n}}

(SC11)

v^{b}_{\Phi_{n}}\leftrightarrow v^{b}_{\Psi_{n}}

(SC12)

For every formula
\Phi_{a}
which consists of a single atom
X
:

v_{\Phi_{a}}^{t}\leftrightarrow X_{t}

(SC13)

v_{\Phi_{a}}^{f}\leftrightarrow X_{f}

(SC14)

v_{\Phi_{a}}^{b}\leftrightarrow X_{b}

(SC15)

For every formula
A\in\mathcal{K}
:

v_{A}^{t}\lor v_{A}^{b}

(SC16)

Let
\mathsf{At}^{\mathcal{K}}_{b}=\{X_{b}\mid X\in\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})\}
. Cardinality constraint:

\mathrm{at\_most}(u,\mathsf{At}^{\mathcal{K}}_{b})

(SC17)

Theorem
1
.

For a given value
u
, the encoding
S_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is satisfiable if and only if
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{c}}}(\mathcal{K})\leq u
.

Proof.

Assume
S_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is satisfiable and let
\omega
be a model of
S_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{K},u)
. Define the three-valued interpretation
\omega^{3}
via

\displaystyle\omega^{3}(X)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}t&\text{if~}\omega(X_{t})=t\\
f&\text{if~}\omega(X_{f})=t\\
b&\text{if~}\omega(X_{b})=t\end{array}\right.

Due to (SC3) (see Encoding
1
) it is clear that
\omega^{3}
is well-defined. We now show, via structural induction, that
\omega^{3}(\Phi)=\theta
iff
\omega(v^{\theta}_{\Phi})=t
for all
\Phi\in\mathsf{sub}(\mathcal{K})
:

•

\Phi_{a}=X
for
X\in\mathsf{At}
: this follows already from the definition of
\omega^{3}
above and (SC13)–(SC15).

•

\Phi=\neg\Psi
: by induction hypothesis,
\omega^{3}(\Psi)=\theta
iff
\omega(v^{\theta}_{\Psi})=t
.
This implies that exactly one of
v^{t}_{\Psi}
,
v^{f}_{\Psi}
,
v^{b}_{\Psi}
is true.
Due to (SC10)–(SC12), it follows that exactly one of
v^{t}_{\neg\Psi}
,
v^{f}_{\neg\Psi}
,
v^{b}_{\neg\Psi}
is true.
Observe that (SC10)–(SC12) models exactly the semantics of negation in three-valued logic.
It follows
\omega^{3}(\neg\Psi)=\theta
iff
\omega(v^{\theta}_{\neg\Psi})=t
.

•

\Phi=\Psi_{1}\wedge\Psi_{2}
: by induction hypothesis,
\omega^{3}(\Psi_{1})=\theta
iff
\omega(v^{\theta}_{\Psi_{1}})=t
and
\omega^{3}(\Psi_{2})=\theta
iff
\omega(v^{\theta}_{\Psi_{2}})=t
.
This implies that exactly one of
v^{t}_{\Psi_{1}}
,
v^{f}_{\Psi_{1}}
,
v^{b}_{\Psi_{1}}
and exactly one of
v^{t}_{\Psi_{2}}
,
v^{f}_{\Psi_{2}}
,
v^{b}_{\Psi_{2}}
is true.
Due to (SC4)–(SC6), it follows that exactly one of
v^{t}_{\Psi_{1}\wedge\Psi_{2}}
,
v^{f}_{\Psi_{1}\wedge\Psi_{2}}
,
v^{b}_{\Psi_{1}\wedge\Psi_{2}}
is true.
Observe that (SC4)–(SC6) models exactly the semantics of conjunction in three-valued logic: (SC4) models the case that both
\Psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{2}
are
true
(then
\Psi_{1}\wedge\Psi_{2}
is also
true
), (SC5) models the case that one of
\Psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{2}
is
false
(then
\Psi_{1}\wedge\Psi_{2}
is also
false
), and (SC6) covers all remaining cases (then
\Psi_{1}\wedge\Psi_{2}
is
both
).

•

\Phi=\Psi_{1}\vee\Psi_{2}
: by induction hypothesis,
\omega^{3}(\Psi_{1})=\theta
iff
\omega(v^{\theta}_{\Psi_{1}})=t
and
\omega^{3}(\Psi_{2})=\theta
iff
\omega(v^{\theta}_{\Psi_{2}})=t
.
This implies that exactly one of
v^{t}_{\Psi_{1}}
,
v^{f}_{\Psi_{1}}
,
v^{b}_{\Psi_{1}}
and exactly one of
v^{t}_{\Psi_{2}}
,
v^{f}_{\Psi_{2}}
,
v^{b}_{\Psi_{2}}
is true.
Due to (SC7)–(SC9), it follows that exactly one of
v^{t}_{\Psi_{1}\vee\Psi_{2}}
,
v^{f}_{\Psi_{1}\vee\Psi_{2}}
,
v^{b}_{\Psi_{1}\vee\Psi_{2}}
is true.
Observe that (SC7)–(SC9) models exactly the semantics of disjunction in three-valued logic: (SC7) models the case that at least one of
\Psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{2}
is
true
(then
\Psi_{1}\vee\Psi_{2}
is also
true
), (SC8) models the case that both
\Psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{2}
are
false
(then
\Psi_{1}\vee\Psi_{2}
is also
false
), and (SC9) covers all remaining cases (then
\Psi_{1}\vee\Psi_{2}
is
both
).

So it follows that
\omega^{3}(\Phi)=\theta
iff
\omega(v^{\theta}_{\Phi})=t
for all
\Phi\in\mathsf{sub}(\mathcal{K})
. Note that this includes all
\Phi\in\mathcal{K}
as well.
Due to (SC16), for all
\Phi\in\mathcal{K}
we have
\omega^{3}(\Phi)\in\{t,b\}
and therefore
\omega^{3}\in\textsf{Mod}^{3}(\mathcal{K})
.
Due to (SC17), we also have
|\textsf{Conflictbase}(\omega^{3})|\leq u
and it follows
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{c}}}(\mathcal{K})\leq u
.

For the other direction, assume
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{c}}}(\mathcal{K})\leq u
. Then there exists a three-valued interpretation
\omega^{3}
with
\omega^{3}\in\textsf{Mod}^{3}(\mathcal{K})
and
|\textsf{Conflictbase}(\omega^{3})|\leq u
. Define
\omega
on the signature of
S_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{K},u)
via

\displaystyle\omega(x_{t})

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}t&\text{if~}\omega^{3}(X)=t\\
f&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\omega(x_{f})

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}t&\text{if~}\omega^{3}(X)=f\\
f&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\omega(x_{b})

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}t&\text{if~}\omega^{3}(X)=b\\
f&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\omega(v^{t}_{\Phi})

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}t&\text{if~}\omega^{3}(\Phi)=t\\
f&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\omega(v^{f}_{\Phi})

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}t&\text{if~}\omega^{3}(\Phi)=f\\
f&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\omega(v^{b}_{\Phi})

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}t&\text{if~}\omega^{3}(\Phi)=b\\
f&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

for all
X\in\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
and
\Phi\in\mathsf{sub}(\mathcal{K})
. Using the same argumentation as above, it can be shown that
\omega
satisfies
S_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{K},u)
.
∎

A.1.2
The Forgetting-Based Inconsistency Measure

Encoding 2:
Overview of the SAT encoding
S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K},u)
of the forgetting-based inconsistency measure
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{f}}
.

\mathcal{K}
is a knowledge base and
\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
its signature.

u
is a candidate for an upper limit for
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K})
.
Let
\mathsf{Occ}(\mathcal{K})
be the set of all atom occurrences in
\mathcal{K}
as defined in Section
3.1
.
The signature size of the encoding is
|\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})|+2\cdot|\mathsf{Occ}(\mathcal{K})|
.

Signature

We make use of the original signature
\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
.

For every occurrence
X^{l}\in\mathsf{Occ}(\mathcal{K})
:

Create atoms
t_{X,l}
,
f_{X,l}

(SF1)

Constraints

For every formula
A\in\mathcal{K}
, replace every atom occurrence
X^{l}
with:

(t_{X,l}\lor X)\land\neg f_{X,l}

(SF2)

Let
\mathsf{At}_{\mathrm{forget}}
be the set of all atoms defined by (SF3).

For each pair
t_{X,l},f_{X,l}\in\mathsf{At}_{\mathrm{forget}}
:

\neg t_{X,l}\lor\neg f_{X,l}

(SF4)

Cardinality constraint:

\mathrm{at\_most}(u,\mathsf{At}_{\mathrm{forget}})

(SF4)

Let
\mathcal{K}
be an arbitrary knowledge base and
S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K},u)
the corresponding SAT encoding defined via (SF1)–(SF4).
Assume that
S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is satisfiable, and let
\omega
be a model of
S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K},u)
.
We define

\displaystyle T_{S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K},u)}(\omega)=

\displaystyle\{t_{X,l}\mid\omega(t_{X,l})=t\}

\displaystyle F_{S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K},u)}(\omega)=

\displaystyle\{f_{X,l}\mid\omega(f_{X,l})=t\}

Due to (SF3) (i. e.,
\lnot t_{X,l}\lor\lnot f_{X,l}
),
T_{S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K},u)}(\omega)\cap F_{S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K},u)}(\omega)=\emptyset
for every model
\omega
of
S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K},u)
.

Lemma 1
.

If
\omega
is a model of
S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K},u)
then
(\bigwedge\mathcal{K})[X^{1}_{1}\rightarrow\top;\ldots;X^{n}_{p}\rightarrow\top;Y^{1}_{1}\rightarrow\bot;\ldots;Y^{m}_{q}\rightarrow\bot]
is consistent with
T_{S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K},u)}(\omega)=\{t_{X_{1},1},\ldots,t_{X_{p},n}\}
and
F_{S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K},u)}(\omega)=\{f_{Y_{1},1},\ldots,f_{Y_{q},m}\}
.

Proof.

To begin with, each
X^{l}
represents exactly the
l
-th occurrence of an atom
X
. Moreover, each
X^{l}
gets a distinct pair of atoms
t_{X,l}
and
f_{X,l}
(SF1).
Thus,
t_{X,l}
and
f_{X,l}
represent exactly the
l
-th occurrence of
X
.

Following (SF2), each atom occurrence
X^{l}
is replaced by the term

(t_{X,l}\lor X)\land\lnot f_{X,l}
.
If
\omega(t_{X,l})=t
(and
\omega(f_{X,l})=f
due to (SF3)), the term becomes true, regardless of whether
\omega(X)=t
or
\omega(X)=f
.
Hence, this corresponds exactly to replacing the
l
-th occurrence of
X
in the original formula by
\top
.
On the other hand, if
\omega(f_{X,l})=t
(and
\omega(t_{X,l})=f
due to (SF3)), the term becomes false, regardless of the truth value of
X
.
Consequently, this corresponds to replacing the
l
-th occurrence of
X
in the original formula by
\bot
.
If
\omega(t_{X,l})=f
and
\omega(f_{X,l})=f
, the right part of the conjunction is true, and the left part is true if
\omega(X)=t
and false if
\omega(X)=f
.
Thus, the term evaluates to the truth value of
X^{l}
, i. e., the truth value of the
l
-th occurrence of
X
in the original formula.
∎

Theorem
2
.

For a given value
u
, the encoding
S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is satisfiable if and only if
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K})\leq u
.

Proof.

From Lemma
1
we know that
T_{S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K},u)}(\omega)
and
F_{S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K},u)}(\omega)
contain exactly those
t_{X,l}
and
f_{X,l}
which correspond to those occurrences of
X
that are being forgotten (i. e., replaced by
\top
, and
\bot
, respectively).
Moreover, the cardinality constraint (SF4) ensures that
|T_{S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K},u)}(\omega)\cup F_{S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K},u)}(\omega)|\leq u
.
Thus, if more than
u
atom occurrences must be forgotten in order for
\mathcal{K}
to be satisfiable, then
S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is unsatisfiable.
Otherwise, i. e., if a minimum of
\leq u
atom occurrences must be forgotten,
S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is satisfiable.
∎

A.1.3
The Hitting-Set Inconsistency Measure

Encoding 3:
Overview of the SAT encoding
S_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathcal{K},u)
of the hitting set inconsistency measure
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}
.

\mathcal{K}
is a knowledge base and
\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
its signature.

u
is a candidate for an upper limit for
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}(\mathcal{K})
.
The signature size of the encoding is
u\cdot\lvert\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})\rvert+u\cdot\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert
.

Signature

For every atom
X\in\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
:

Create variables
X_{i}
with
i\in\{1,\ldots,u\}

(SH1)

For every formula
A\in\mathcal{K}
:

Create variables
p_{A,i}
with
i\in\{1,\ldots,u\}

(SH2)

Constraints

For every formula
A\in\mathcal{K}
and
i\in\{1,\ldots,u\}
, create
A_{i}
, which is a copy of
A

where each instance of atom
X
is replaced by
X_{i}
, and add:

p_{A,i}\rightarrow A_{i}

(SH3)

For every formula
A\in\mathcal{K}
:

\bigvee_{1\leq i\leq u}p_{A,i}

(SH4)

Let
\mathcal{K}
be an arbitrary knowledge base and
S_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathcal{K},u)
the corresponding SAT encoding defined via (SH1)–(SH4).

Lemma 2
.

If
\mathcal{K}
contains at least one contradictory formula,
S_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is unsatisfiable for all
u\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathcal{K}|\}
.

Proof.

Let
u\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathcal{K}|\}
be arbitrary, and let some
A^{\bot}\in\mathcal{K}
be contradictory.
Then there exists no interpretation which could satisfy a copy
A^{\bot}_{i}
(
i\in\{1,\ldots,u\}
) of
A^{\bot}
.
Hence, in order to satisfy the constraints defined by (SH3) (i. e.,
p_{A^{\bot},i}\rightarrow A^{\bot}_{i}
), each
p_{A^{\bot},i}
would need to evaluate to false.
However, due to (SH4), at least one
p_{A^{\bot},i}
must be true.
Thus,
S_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is unsatisfiable, regardless of the value of
u
.
∎

Assume that
\mathcal{K}
does not contain any contradictory formulas and that
S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is satisfiable, and let
\omega
be a model of
S_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathcal{K},u)
.
We define

I_{S_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathcal{K},u)}(\omega)=\{i_{a}\mid\exists\omega(p_{A,i_{a}})=t\}

with
A\in\mathcal{K}
, and
i_{a}\in\{1,\ldots,u\}
.

Lemma 3
.

I_{S_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathcal{K},u)}(\omega)
corresponds to a hitting set of
\mathcal{K}
.

Proof.

For each formula
A\in\mathcal{K}
we have at least one
p_{A,i_{a}}
that evaluates to true, because of (SH4).
Consequently, wrt. that
p_{A,i_{a}}
,
A_{i_{a}}
must be true as well (SH3).
Hence, at least one copy
A_{i_{a}}
of each
A\in\mathcal{K}
is satisfied.

Further, each
i\in\{1,\ldots,u\}
corresponds to an interpretation, since each
A_{i}
uses the
i
-th copy of
\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
.
Moreover, each
i_{a}\in I_{S_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathcal{K},u)}
corresponds to an “active” interpretation, i. e., one that is actually used to satisfy one or more formulas.
Thus,
I_{S_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathcal{K},u)}(\omega)
corresponds to a hitting set of
\mathcal{K}
.
∎

Theorem
3
.

For a given value
u
, the encoding
S_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is satisfiable if and only if
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}(\mathcal{K})\leq u-1
.

S_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is unsatisfiable for all
u=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{K}|
if and only if
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}(\mathcal{K})=\infty
.

Proof.

Lemma
2
shows that
S_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is unsatisfiable for all
u\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathcal{K}|\}
if
\mathcal{K}
contains a contradictory formula, i. e., if
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}(\mathcal{K})=\infty
.
Lemma
3
shows that, if
\mathcal{K}
does not contain any contradictory formula,
I_{S_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathcal{K},u)}(\omega)=\{i_{a}\mid\exists\omega(p_{A,i_{a}})=t\}
corresponds to a hitting set of
\mathcal{K}
.
Since the maximum cardinality of
I_{S_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathcal{K},u)}(\omega)
is restricted by
u
,
S_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is unsatisfiable if more than
u
copies of each formula are required, i. e., if more than
u
different interpretations are required to satisfy each formula in
\mathcal{K}
.
Otherwise,
S_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is satisfiable.

Thus, if a hitting set of size
\leq u
exists,
S_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is satisfiable.
Otherwise, i. e., if no hitting set of size
\leq u
exists,
S_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is unsatisfiable.
Since we have to subtract
1
from the cardinality of the hitting set in order to get
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}(\mathcal{K})
, it follows that if
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}(\mathcal{K})\leq u-1
, then
S_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is satisfiable, and if
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{h}}}(\mathcal{K})>u-1
, then
S_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is unsatisfiable.
∎

A.1.4
The Max-Distance Inconsistency Measure

Encoding 4:
Overview of the SAT encoding
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K},u)
of the max-distance inconsistency measure
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}
.

\mathcal{K}
is a knowledge base and
\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
its signature.

u
is a candidate for an upper limit for
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K})
.
The signature size of the encoding is
\lvert\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})\rvert+2\cdot\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert\cdot\lvert\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})\rvert
.

Signature

For every atom
X\in\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
:

Create variable
X_{o}

(SDM1)

For every atom
X\in\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
and
i\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathcal{K}|\}
:

Create variables
X_{i}

(SDM2)

Create variables
\textsf{inv}_{X,i}

(SDM3)

Constraints

For every formula
A\in\mathcal{K}
and
i\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathcal{K}|\}
:

Create
A_{i}
, which is a copy of
A
where each instance of atom
X
is replaced by
X_{i}

(SDM4)

For every
X_{i}
with
i\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathcal{K}|\}
:

X_{i}\rightarrow X_{o}\lor\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}

(SDM5)

\lnot X_{i}\rightarrow\lnot X_{o}\lor\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}

(SDM6)

Let
\textsf{INV}_{i}
be the set containing all
\textsf{inv}_{X,i}
for a fixed
i
.

For every
i\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathcal{K}|\}
:

\mathrm{at\_most}(u,\textsf{INV}_{i})

(SDM7)

Let
\mathcal{K}
be an arbitrary knowledge base and
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K},u)
the corresponding SAT encoding defined via (SDM1)–(SDM7).

Lemma 4
.

If
\mathcal{K}
contains at least one contradictory formula,
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K},u)
is unsatisfiable for all
u\in\{0,\ldots,|\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})|\}
.

Proof.

Let some
A^{\bot}\in\mathcal{K}
be contradictory.
Following (SDM4), we add indexed copies of all formulas
A\in\mathcal{K}
to
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K},u)
.
Thus, we also have an indexed copy
A^{\bot}_{i}
of
A^{\bot}
.
Since adding an index
i
to each atom in
A
does not resolve the conflict within the formula,
A^{\bot}_{i}
is still contradictory.
Therefore,
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K},u)
contains an unsatisfiable formula and is consequently itself unsatisfiable, regardless of the value of
u
.
∎

Assume that
\mathcal{K}
does not contain any contradictory formulas and that
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K},u)
is satisfiable, and let
\omega
be a model of
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K},u)
.
For each
i\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathcal{K}|\}
we create variables
X_{i}
(SDM2) and variables
\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}
(SDM3) for each
X\in\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
.
We define

I_{\max}(\omega,i)=\{\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}\mid\omega(\mathsf{inv}_{X,i})=t\}

Recall that we represent an “optimal” interpretation by introducing a variable
X_{o}
for each
X\in\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
(SDM1).
The truth values assigned to each
X_{o}
then correspond to the “optimal” interpretation.

Lemma 5
.

If
\omega(X_{i})\neq\omega(X_{o})
then
\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}\in I_{\max}(\omega,i)
.

Proof.

X_{i}
must be either true or false.
If
\omega(X_{i})=t
, then the left part of the implication
X_{i}\rightarrow X_{o}\lor\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}
(SDM5) is true.
Thus, in order to satisfy the implication, the right part must be true as well.
If
X_{o}
is false, i. e.,
\omega(X_{i})\neq\omega(X_{o})
, then
\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}
must be true.
Consequently,
\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}\in I_{\max}(\omega,i)
.
Moreover, the implication
\lnot X_{i}\rightarrow\lnot X_{o}\lor\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}
, defined via (SDM6), evaluates to true as well, because the left part (
\lnot X_{i}
) is false.

If
\omega(X_{i})=f
, the left part of the implication defined by (SDM6), i. e.,
\lnot X_{i}\rightarrow\lnot X_{o}\lor\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}
, is true.
Thus, to satisfy this implication,
\lnot X_{o}\lor\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}
must evaluate to true.
If
\omega(X_{o})=t
, meaning if
\omega(X_{i})\neq\omega(X_{o})
, then
\lnot X_{o}
is false, and
\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}
must be true.
Hence,
\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}\in I_{\max}(\omega,i)
.
In addition, the implication
X_{i}\rightarrow X_{o}\lor\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}
, defined by (SDM5), is also satisfied, because the left part (
X_{i}
) is false.
∎

Theorem
4
.

For a given value
u
, the encoding
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K},u)
is satisfiable if and only if
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K})\leq u
.

S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K},u)
is unsatisfiable for all
u\in\{0,\ldots,\lvert\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})\rvert\}
if and only if
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K})=\infty
.

Proof.

Lemma
4
shows that
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K},u)
is unsatisfiable for all
u\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})|\}
if
\mathcal{K}
contains a contradictory formula, i. e., if
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K})=\infty
.

Let us now assume that
\mathcal{K}
does not contain any contradictory formulas, i. e.,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K})\neq\infty
.
From Lemma
5
we know that for each
i\in|\mathcal{K}|
, if
\omega(X_{i})\neq\omega(X_{o})
, then the corresponding
\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}
must be included in
I_{\max}(\omega,i)
(i. e.,
\omega(\mathsf{inv}_{X,i})=t
).
This means that if a formula
A\in\mathcal{K}
with index
i
requires an interpretation that differs from the “optimal” interpretation, then for each
X_{i}\in\mathsf{At}(A_{i})
with
\omega(X_{i})\neq\omega(X_{o})
,
\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}\in I_{\max}(\omega,i)
.
Further, (SDM7) restricts
|I_{\max}(\omega,i)|
to
u
(for each
i
).
If
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K})\leq u
, there exists a solution for which
|I_{\max}(\omega,i)|\leq u
for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathcal{K}|\}
.

Hence, the constraints defined by (SDM7) are satisfied, and
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K},u)
is satisfiable.
If
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K})>u
, then for at least one
i
we have
|I_{\max}(\omega,i)|>u
.
Consequently, at least one constraint defined by (SDM7) cannot be satisfied, which makes
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}(\mathcal{K},u)
unsatisfiable.
∎

A.1.5
The Sum-Distance Inconsistency Measure

Encoding 5:
Overview of the SAT encoding
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K},u)
of the sum-distance inconsistency measure
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}
.

\mathcal{K}
is a knowledge base and
\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
its signature.

u
is a candidate for an upper limit for
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K})
.
The signature size of the encoding is
\lvert\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})\rvert+2\cdot\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert\cdot\lvert\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})\rvert
.

Signature

For every atom
X\in\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
:

Create variable
X_{o}

(SDS1)

For every atom
X\in\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
and
i\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathcal{K}|\}
:

Create variables
X_{i}

(SDS2)

Create variables
\textsf{inv}_{X,i}

(SDS3)

Constraints

For every formula
A\in\mathcal{K}
and
i\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathcal{K}|\}
:

Create
A_{i}
, which is a copy of
A
where each instance of atom
X
is replaced by
X_{i}

(SDS4)

For every
X_{i}
with
i\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathcal{K}|\}
:

X_{i}\rightarrow X_{o}\vee\textsf{inv}_{X,i}

(SDS5)

\lnot X_{i}\rightarrow\neg X_{o}\vee\textsf{inv}_{X,i}

(SDS6)

Let
INV
be the set containing all
\textsf{inv}_{X,i}
for all
i
. Cardinality constraint:

\mathrm{at\_most}(u,\textsf{INV})

(SDS7)

Let
\mathcal{K}
be an arbitrary knowledge base and
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K},u)
the corresponding SAT encoding defined via (SDS1)–(SDS7).

Lemma 6
.

If
\mathcal{K}
contains at least one contradictory formula,
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K},u)
is unsatisfiable for all
u\in\{0,\ldots,|\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})|\cdot|\mathcal{K}|\}
.

Proof.

Analogous to the proof of Lemma
4
.
∎

Assume that
\mathcal{K}
does not contain any contradictory formulas and that
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K},u)
is satisfiable, and let
\omega
be a model of
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K},u)
.
For each
i\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathcal{K}|\}
we create variables
X_{i}
(SDS2) and variables
\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}
(SDS3) for each
X\in\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
.
We define

I_{\Sigma}(\omega)=\{\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}\mid\omega(\mathsf{inv}_{X,i})=t\}

Recall that we represent an “optimal” interpretation by introducing a variable
X_{o}
for each
X\in\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
(SDS1).
The truth values assigned to each
X_{o}
then correspond to the “optimal” interpretation.

Lemma 7
.

If
\omega(X_{i})\neq\omega(X_{o})
then
\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}\in I_{\Sigma}(\omega)
.

Proof.

Analogous to the proof of Lemma
5
, since (SDM1)–(SDM6) is equivalent to (SDS1)–(SDS6).
∎

Theorem
5
.

For a given value
u
, the encoding
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K},u)
is satisfiable if and only if
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K})\leq u
.

S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K},u)
is unsatisfiable for all
u\in\{0,\ldots,\lvert\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})\rvert\cdot\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert\}
if and only if
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K})=\infty
.

Proof.

Lemma
6
shows that
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K},u)
is unsatisfiable for all
u\in\{0,\ldots,|\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})|\cdot|\mathcal{K}|\}
if
\mathcal{K}
contains a contradictory formula, i. e., if
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K})=\infty
.

Let us now assume that
\mathcal{K}
does not contain any contradictory formulas, i. e.,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K})\neq\infty
.
From Lemma
7
we know that for any
i\in|\mathcal{K}|
, if
\omega(X_{i})\neq\omega(X_{o})
, then the corresponding
\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}
must be included in
I_{\Sigma}(\omega)
(i. e.,
\omega(\mathsf{inv}_{X,i})=t
).
This means that if a formula
A\in\mathcal{K}
with index
i
requires an interpretation that differs from the “optimal” interpretation, then for each
X_{i}\in\mathsf{At}(A_{i})
with
\omega(X_{i})\neq\omega(X_{o})
,
\mathsf{inv}_{X,i}\in I_{\Sigma}(\omega)
.
Further, (SDS7) restricts
|I_{\Sigma}(\omega)|
to
u
.
If
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K})\leq u
, there exists a solution for which
|I_{\Sigma}(\omega)|\leq u
.
Hence, the constraint defined by (SDS7) is satisfied, and
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K},u)
is satisfiable.
If
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K})>u
, then we have
|I_{\Sigma}(\omega)|>u
.
Consequently, the constraint defined by (SDS7) cannot be satisfied, which makes
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\Sigma}(\mathcal{K},u)
unsatisfiable.
∎

A.1.6
The Hit-Distance Inconsistency Measure

Encoding 6:
Overview of the SAT encoding
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\mathrm{hit}}(\mathcal{K},u)
of the hit-distance inconsistency measure
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\text{hit}}
.

\mathcal{K}
is a knowledge base and
\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
its signature.

u
is a candidate for an upper limit for
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\text{hit}}(\mathcal{K})
.
The signature size of the encoding is
\lvert\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})\rvert+\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert
.

Signature

For every formula
A\in\mathcal{K}
:

Create variable
\textsf{hit}_{A}

(SDH1)

For every atom
X\in\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
:

Create variable
X

(SDH2)

Constraints

For every formula
A\in\mathcal{K}
:

A\lor\textsf{hit}_{A}

(SDH3)

Let
\textsf{HIT}_{\mathcal{K}}
be the set containing all variables added in (SDH1).

Cardinality constraint:

\mathrm{at\_most}(u,\textsf{HIT}_{\mathcal{K}})

(SDH4)

Let
\mathcal{K}
be an arbitrary knowledge base and
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\mathrm{hit}}(\mathcal{K},u)
the corresponding SAT encoding defined via (SDH1)–(SDH4).
Assume that
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\mathrm{hit}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is satisfiable, and let
\omega
be a model of
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\mathrm{hit}}(\mathcal{K},u)
.
We define

H_{\mathsf{hit}}(\omega)=\{\mathsf{hit}_{A}\mid\omega(\mathsf{hit}_{A})=t\}.

Lemma 8
.

Each
\mathsf{hit}_{A}\in H_{\mathsf{hit}}(\omega)
corresponds to a formula
A\in\mathcal{K}
being removed from
\mathcal{K}
.

Proof.

Due to (SDH3), every formula
A\in\mathcal{K}
is extended by “
\lor\ \mathsf{hit}_{A}
”.
If
\omega(\mathsf{hit}_{A})=t
, the term
A\lor\mathsf{hit}_{A}
will always be true, regardless of the truth value of
A
under
\omega
.
Hence, this is equivalent to the removal of
A
from
\mathcal{K}
.
∎

Theorem
6
.

For a given value
u
, the encoding
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\mathrm{hit}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is satisfiable if and only if
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\text{hit}}(\mathcal{K})\leq u
.

Proof.

The cardinality constraint (SDH4) restricts the number of atoms
\mathsf{hit}_{A}
being allowed to be true, to
u
.
If the minimal number of formulas
A\in\mathcal{K}
that need to be removed in order to make
\mathcal{K}
consistent is
\leq u
(i. e., if
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\text{hit}}(\mathcal{K})\leq u
) then
\leq u
atoms
\mathsf{hit}_{A}
can be set to true (i. e.,
|H_{\mathsf{hit}}(\omega)|\leq u
).
From Lemma
8
we know that those atoms correspond to the formulas that are removed.
Thus, there exists a solution in which exactly those atoms
\mathsf{hit}_{A}
are set to true which correspond to the formulas
A\in\mathcal{K}
that need to be removed to make
\mathcal{K}
consistent.
Consequently, the remaining formulas in
\mathcal{K}
are satisfiable, and
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\mathrm{hit}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is satisfiable.
If more than
u
formulas need to be removed (i. e., if
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\text{hit}}(\mathcal{K})>u
), (SDH4) still restricts the number of
\mathsf{hit}_{A}
atoms being set to true to
u
.
Hence, there exists no model for
\{\bigcup A\in\mathcal{K}\mid\omega(\mathsf{hit}_{A})=f\}
, and
S_{\mathrm{d}}^{\mathrm{hit}}(\mathcal{K},u)
is unsatisfiable.
∎

A.2
ASP-based Algorithms

The following sections are each comprised of a correctness proof corresponding to the ASP encoding for one of the six inconsistency measures considered in this work.

A.2.1
The Contension Inconsistency Measure

Encoding 7:
Overview of the ASP encoding
P_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{K})
of the contension inconsistency measure
\mathcal{I}{{}_{\mathrm{c}}}
.
\mathcal{K}
is the given knowledge base.

For every
A\in\mathcal{K}
:

\texttt{kbMember(}A\texttt{).}

(AC1)

For every
X\in\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
:

\texttt{atom(}X\texttt{).}

(AC2)

For every conjunction
\Phi_{c}=\Psi_{c,1}\land\Psi_{c,2}
appearing in some formula:

\texttt{conjunction(}\Phi_{c}\texttt{,}\Psi_{c,1}\texttt{,}\Psi_{c,2}\texttt{).}

(AC3)

For every disjunction
\Phi_{d}=\Psi_{d,1}\lor\Psi_{d,2}
appearing in some formula:

\texttt{disjunction(}\Phi_{d}\texttt{,}\Psi_{d,1}\texttt{,}\Psi_{d,2}\texttt{).}

(AC4)

For each negation
\Phi_{n}=\lnot\Psi_{n}
appearing in some formula:

\texttt{negation(}\Phi_{n}\texttt{,}\Psi_{n}\texttt{).}

(AC5)

For every formula
\Phi_{a}
which consists of a single atom
X
:

\texttt{formulaIsAtom(}\Phi_{a}\texttt{,}X\texttt{).}

(AC6)

As the static part, we define:

\texttt{tv(}t\texttt{;}f\texttt{;}b\texttt{).}

(AC7)

1{truthValue(A,T) : tv(T)}1 :-

atom(A).

) :-

),

).

)}.

)}.

),

(AC19)

#minimize{1,A : truthValue(A,

), atom(A)}.

(AC20)

Consider an arbitrary knowledge base
\mathcal{K}
as well as the set of rules listed in Encoding
7
in Section
4.2
, which make up the extended logic program
P_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{K})
.
Let
P_{\mathrm{c}}^{\prime}(\mathcal{K})
denote the extended logic program
P_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{K})

without
the minimize statement (AC20).

Lemma 9
.

If
M
is an answer set of
P_{\mathrm{c}}^{\prime}(\mathcal{K})
then the three-valued interpretation
\omega^{3}_{M}
defined as

\displaystyle\omega^{3}_{M}(X)=\begin{cases}t&\quad\mathtt{truthValue(}X,t\mathtt{)}\in M\\
f&\quad\mathtt{truthValue(}X,f\mathtt{)}\in M\\
b&\quad\mathtt{truthValue(}X,b\mathtt{)}\in M\end{cases}

is a model of
\mathcal{K}
with
X\in\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
.

Proof.

\omega^{3}_{M}
is well-defined:
As (AC8) states, each answer set of
P_{\mathrm{c}}^{\prime}(\mathcal{K})
will hold exactly one of
\texttt{truthValue(}X\texttt{,}t\texttt{)}
,
\texttt{truthValue(}X\texttt{,}f\texttt{)}
,
\texttt{truthValue(}X\texttt{,}b\texttt{)}
for each atom
X\in\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
:

\displaystyle 1\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace truthValue(}X\texttt{,T) : tv(T)\lx@text@rbrace}1\texttt{ :- atom(}X\texttt{).}

Via structural induction, we show that for all
(sub)
formulas
\Phi
in
\mathcal{K}
,
\omega^{3}_{M}(\Phi)=\theta
(with
\theta\in\{t,f,b\}
) if and only if
\texttt{truthValue(}\Phi\texttt{,}\theta\texttt{)}\in M
.

Induction Hypothesis (I.H.):

\omega^{3}_{M}(\Phi)=\theta
if and only if
\texttt{truthValue(}\Phi\texttt{,}\theta\texttt{)}\in M
is true for all subformulas of the considered formula.

1.

Let
\Phi=X
for
X\in\mathsf{At}(\mathcal{K})
.

•

“
\Rightarrow
”:

\omega^{3}_{M}(\Phi)=\theta\Rightarrow\omega^{3}_{M}(X)=\theta\xRightarrow{\text{Def.}}{}\texttt{truthValue(}X\texttt{,}\theta\texttt{)}\in M

•

“
\Leftarrow
”:

\texttt{truthValue(}X\texttt{,}\theta\texttt{)}\in M\xRightarrow{\text{Def.}}{}\omega^{3}_{M}(X)=\theta\Rightarrow\omega^{3}_{M}(\Phi)=\theta

2.

Let
\Phi=\Psi_{1}\land\Psi_{2}
.

(a)

\theta=t
:

•

“
\Rightarrow
”:

\displaystyle\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1}\land\Psi_{2})=\theta

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\theta=t}{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1})=t\\
\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{2})=t\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\text{I.H.}}{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\texttt{,}t\texttt{)}\in M\\
\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}t\texttt{)}\in M\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\text{(AC9)}}{}\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\land\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}t\texttt{)}\in M

•

“
\Leftarrow
”:

\displaystyle\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\land\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}t\texttt{)}\in M

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\texttt{,}t\texttt{)}\in M\\
\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}t\texttt{)}\in M\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1})=t\\
\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{2})=t\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1}\land\Psi_{2})=t

(b)

\theta=b
:

•

“
\Rightarrow
”:

\displaystyle\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1}\land\Psi_{2})=\theta

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\theta=b}{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1})=t\\
\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{2})=b\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\text{I.H.}}{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\texttt{,}t\texttt{)}\in M\\
\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}b\texttt{)}\in M\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\text{(AC11)}}{}\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\land\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}b\texttt{)}\in M

Analogous for the case where
\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1})=b
and
\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{2})=t
.

or

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\theta=b}{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1})=b\\
\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{2})=b\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\text{I.H.}}{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\texttt{,}b\texttt{)}\in M\\
\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}b\texttt{)}\in M\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\text{(AC11)}}{}\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\land\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}b\texttt{)}\in M

•

“
\Leftarrow
”:

\displaystyle\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\land\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}b\texttt{)}\in M

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\texttt{,}t\texttt{)}\in M\\
\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}b\texttt{)}\in M\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1})=t\\
\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{2})=b\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1}\land\Psi_{2})=b

Analogous for the case where
\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1})=b
and
\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{2})=t
.

or

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\texttt{,}b\texttt{)}\in M\\
\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}b\texttt{)}\in M\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1})=b\\
\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{2})=b\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1}\land\Psi_{2})=b

(c)

\theta=f
:

•

“
\Rightarrow
”:

\displaystyle\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1}\land\Psi_{2})=\theta

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\theta=f}{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1})=t\\
\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{2})=f\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\text{I.H.}}{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\texttt{,}t\texttt{)}\in M\\
\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{f}\texttt{,}f\texttt{)}\in M\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\text{(AC10)}}{}\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\land\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}f\texttt{)}\in M

Analogous for the case where
\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1})=f
and
\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{2})=t
.

or

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\theta=f}{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1})=b\\
\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{2})=f\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\text{I.H.}}{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\texttt{,}b\texttt{)}\in M\\
\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}f\texttt{)}\in M\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\text{(AC10)}}{}\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\land\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}f\texttt{)}\in M

Analogous for the case where
\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1})=f
and
\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{2})=b
.

or

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\theta=f}{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1})=f\\
\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{2})=f\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\text{I.H.}}{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\texttt{,}f\texttt{)}\in M\\
\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}f\texttt{)}\in M\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\text{(AC10)}}{}\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\land\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}f\texttt{)}\in M

•

“
\Leftarrow
”:

\displaystyle\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\land\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}f\texttt{)}\in M

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\texttt{,}t\texttt{)}\in M\\
\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}f\texttt{)}\in M\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1})=t\\
\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{2})=f\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1}\land\Psi_{2})=f

Analogous for the case where
\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1})=f
and
\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{2})=t
.

or

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\texttt{,}b\texttt{)}\in M\\
\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}f\texttt{)}\in M\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1})=b\\
\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{2})=f\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1}\land\Psi_{2})=f

Analogous for the case where
\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1})=f
and
\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{2})=b
.

or

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\texttt{,}f\texttt{)}\in M\\
\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}f\texttt{)}\in M\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1})=f\\
\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{2})=f\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1}\land\Psi_{2})=f

3.

\Phi=\Psi_{1}\lor\Psi_{2}

\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}f\texttt{)}\in M\end{array}\right\}

Analogous for the case where

.

\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{2})=b\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\text{I.H.}}{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\texttt{,}t\texttt{)}\in M\\
\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}b\texttt{)}\in M\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\text{(AC13)}}{}\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\lor\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}t\texttt{)}\in M

Analogous for the case where

and

•

\Leftarrow
”:

\displaystyle\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\lor\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}t\texttt{)}\in M

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1})=t\\
\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{2})=f\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1}\lor\Psi_{2})=t

Analogous for the case where

and

.

or

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\texttt{,}t\texttt{)}\in M\\
\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}b\texttt{)}\in M\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1})=t\\

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\omega^{3}_{M}(\Psi_{1}\lor\Psi_{2})=t

\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}f\texttt{)}\in M\end{array}\right\}

and

.

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\text{(AC14)}}{}\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\lor\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}b\texttt{)}\in M

•

“
\Leftarrow
”:

\displaystyle\texttt{truthValue(}\Psi_{1}\lor\Psi_{2}\texttt{,}b\texttt{)}\in M
